## Zechariah - Introduction (id: en_tn_ZEC_yx6e)

Introduction to Zechariah
=========================

Part 1: General Introduction
----------------------------

### Outline of the Book of Zechariah

1. Zechariah calls the returning nation of Israel to repent and return to Yahweh (1:1–6\)
2. The eight night visions (1:7–6:8\)

    1. Yahweh sends his messengers throughout the whole earth (1:7–17\)
        2. Four horns and four craftsmen (1:18–21\)
        3. The measuring line (2:1–13\)
        4. The priest in filthy clothes (3:1–10\)
        5. The golden lampstand olive trees (4:1–14\)
        6. The flying scroll (5:1–4\)
        7. The woman in a basket (5:5–11\)
        8. Four chariots (6:1–8\)
3. A crown for the high priest (6:9–15\)
4. Questions about fasting (7:1–8:23\)
5. Yahweh judge the nations and saves his people (9:1–11:17\)
6. Final message (12:1–14:21\)

    1. Yahweh will finally defeat Israel’s enemies (12:1–9\)
        2. Israel mourns for the one they pierced (12:10–14\)
        3. Yahweh purifies Israel and removes false prophets (13:1–6\)
        4. Yahweh comes as king (14:1–21\)

### Literary Structure

Many interpreters see Zechariah organized using chiasms—mirrored patterns where the beginning and end match, then the second and second\-to\-last parts match, and so on. Three larger sections show this design: the eight night visions (1:7–6:15\), the two fasting messages (7:1–8:23\), and the closing oracles (chapters 9–14\). Smaller chiasms also appear within individual passages, such as 1:14–17 and 8:9–13\.

### What is the Book of Zechariah about?

Zechariah wrote to the people of Judah who returned from being exiled in Babylon. The people of Judah continued to sin, as their ancestors did before they went into exile. Zechariah called the people to repent and to obey Yahweh. And he called them to renew the covenant with Yahweh. Zechariah also prophesied about the Messiah and about Israel finally being victorious over her enemies.

### How should the title of this book be translated?

This book traditionally has been titled “Zechariah” or “The Book of Zechariah.” Translators may decide to call it the “The Book About Zechariah” or “The Sayings of Zechariah.”

### Who wrote the Book of Zechariah?

The prophet Zechariah wrote this book between 520 and 518 B.C.

Part 2: Important Religious and Cultural Concepts
-------------------------------------------------

### Did Zechariah prophesy before Ezra, Nehemiah, and Haggai?

Zechariah prophesied before the time of Ezra and Nehemiah. He prophesied at the same time as Haggai and continued after Haggai.

Part 3: Important Translation Issues
------------------------------------

### What is the meaning of the term “Israel”?

The name “Israel” is used in many different ways in the Bible. Jacob was a son of Isaac. God changed Jacob’s name to Israel. The descendants of Jacob became a nation also called Israel. Eventually, the nation of Israel split into two kingdoms. The northern kingdom was named Israel. The southern kingdom was named Judah. Zechariah uses the term “Israel” to refer to both the nation of Israel and the northern kingdom of Israel.

### What imagery does Zechariah use in reference to the Messiah?

Zechariah presented the Messiah as both a king and a shepherd. Zechariah said that the Messiah would come and deliver his people. However, the Messiah would also be pierced (12:10\) and struck with the sword (13:7\).

### What does the phrase “that day” mean?

Zechariah often spoke of the last days by using the phrase “that day” or “in that day.” When referring to a future “day,” the translator should be aware that the author may be speaking about the last days. If your language wouldn’t use the repetition of “on that day” for stylistic emphasis, find a natural way to do it in your language.

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 0:0


## Zechariah - Chapter 1 Introduction (id: en_tn_ZEC_qki3)

Zechariah 1 General Notes
=========================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

This chapter is written in prose with imagery used throughout to portray Zechariah’s vision given by the Lord.

Some translations prefer to set apart quotations. The ULT and many other English translations set lines 1:3–6, 14–17, which are extended quotations, farther to the right on the page than the rest of the text.

### The Eight Night\-Visions as a Chiasm

Chapters 1:7–6:8 present eight visions arranged in a mirrored (chiastic) pattern: patrolling horses (1:7–17\) match the closing chariot vision (6:1–8\); the horns that scattered Judah (1:18–21\) match the removal of wickedness in the basket vision (5:5–11\); Jerusalem without walls (2:1–5\) matches the scroll of judgment (5:1–4\); and Joshua reclothed (3:1–10\) matches the two anointed leaders, Joshua and Zerubbabel (4:1–14\). This chapter contains the first two panels of that pattern.

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### Visions

This book contains a number of visions given to Zechariah. The images are not ones Zechariah actually saw; rather, they were given to him in a vision or dream. It is important for each of these visions to keep their given meanings and not be interpreted to mean any specific thing.

Translation Issues in This Chapter
----------------------------------

### Date

Zechariah uses two separate dates in this first chapter. When compared to the book of Haggai, Zechariah’s prophecy was proclaimed just a few weeks after the prophecy of Haggai. However, the two men prophesied in two different places: Haggai was in Jerusalem, and Zechariah was somewhere outside of Jerusalem.

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 1:0


## Zechariah 1:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_vcjw)

בַּ⁠חֹ֨דֶשׁ֙ הַ⁠שְּׁמִינִ֔י בִּ⁠שְׁנַ֥ת שְׁתַּ֖יִם לְ⁠דָרְיָ֑וֶשׁ

**"In the eighth month, in the second year of Darius"**

If your language would not represent numbers in a form such as **eighth** and **second**, you could represent these numbers in a form that your language would use. Alternate translation: \[In month eight in year two of Darius]

See: Ordinal Numbers

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 1:1
* **Associated Articles:** Ordinal Numbers (ID: `33393@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Berechiah (ID: `person:Berechiah.6`); Zechariah (ID: `person:Zechariah.17`); Iddo (ID: `person:Iddo.6`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`); Darius (ID: `person:Darius.2`)

## Zechariah 1:1 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_c5as)

הָיָ֣ה דְבַר־יְהוָ֗ה אֶל־זְכַרְיָה֙

**"the word of Yahweh came to Zechariah"**

Zechariah is using **the word of Yahweh came to Zechariah** as a common expression of the culture to mean “Yahweh communicated a message to Zechariah.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[Yahweh spoke his message to Zechariah] or \[Yahweh gave his message to Zechariah]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 1:1
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Berechiah (ID: `person:Berechiah.6`); Zechariah (ID: `person:Zechariah.17`); Iddo (ID: `person:Iddo.6`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`); Darius (ID: `person:Darius.2`)

## Zechariah 1:1 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_bra8)

בַּ⁠חֹ֨דֶשׁ֙ הַ⁠שְּׁמִינִ֔י בִּ⁠שְׁנַ֥ת שְׁתַּ֖יִם לְ⁠דָרְיָ֑וֶשׁ

**"In the eighth month, in the second year of Darius"**

Zechariah is using the phrase **In the eighth month, in the second year of Darius** to introduce a new event in the story (in this case, the very beginning). Use a word, phrase, or other method in your language that is natural for introducing a new event.

See: Introduction of a New Event

## Zechariah 1:2 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_kog5)

קָצַ֧ף \& קָֽצֶף

**"was angry {with} anger"**

Yahweh is using a construction in which a verb and its object come from the same root. You may be able to use the same construction in your language to express the meaning here. Alternatively, your language may have another way of saying this. Alternate translation: \[was extremely angry]

See: Poetry

וְ⁠אָמַרְתָּ֣ אֲלֵ⁠הֶ֗ם כֹּ֤ה אָמַר֙ יְהוָ֣ה צְבָא֔וֹת

## Zechariah 1:3 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_x9ar)

שׁ֣וּבוּ אֵלַ֔⁠י

**"Return to me"**

Here Yahweh is speaking as if the people’s relationship with him were a place that they had physically left and could physically come back to. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[Be faithful to me again] or \[Renew your commitment to me]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 1:3
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Zechariah 1:3 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_gc9s)

נְאֻ֖ם יְהוָ֣ה צְבָא֑וֹת

**"the declaration of Yahweh of Armies"**

The term **the declaration of Yahweh of Armies** introduces a parenthetical affirmation of the source of this direct quotation. In your translation, use a natural way of indicating the authoritative source of direct quotations in your language.

See: Quotations and Quote Margins

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 1:3
* **Associated Articles:** Quotations and Quote Margins (ID: `33426@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 1:3 (#4) (id: en_tn_ZEC_xoxv)

שׁ֣וּבוּ אֵלַ֔⁠י \& וְ⁠אָשׁ֣וּב אֲלֵי⁠כֶ֔ם

**"Return to me \& and I will return to you"**

Here, **Return to me** introduces the first part of a possible condition. Yahweh will only do the thing in the second part, **and I will return to you**, if this condition happens. Use a natural form in your language for introducing a condition that could happen. Alternate translation: \[If you return to me, then I will return to you]

See: Connect — Hypothetical Conditions

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 1:3
* **Associated Articles:** Connect — Hypothetical Conditions (ID: `33327@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Zechariah 1:3 (#5) (id: en_tn_ZEC_lw80)

וְ⁠אָשׁ֣וּב אֲלֵי⁠כֶ֔ם

**"and I will return to you"**

Here Yahweh is speaking as if his renewed favor toward the people were a physical movement back to them. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and I will be favorable to you again] or \[and I will bless you again]

## Zechariah 1:4 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_i8gr)

מִ⁠דַּרְכֵי⁠כֶ֣ם הָ⁠רָעִ֔ים ו⁠מעלילי⁠כם הָֽ⁠רָעִ֑ים

**"from your evil ways and your evil deeds"**

The terms **evil ways** and **evil deeds** mean similar things. Yahweh is using the two terms together for emphasis. If it would be clearer for your readers, you could express the emphasis with a single phrase. Alternate translation: \[from all of your evil conduct]

See: Doublet

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 1:4

**"But they did not hear, and they did not pay attention"**

**"But they did not hear, and they did not pay attention to me"**

## Zechariah 1:5 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_nx2k)

אֲבֽוֹתֵי⁠כֶ֖ם אַיֵּה־הֵ֑ם

**"Your fathers, where {are} they"**

Yahweh is using the question form to emphasize that the ancestors are dead. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[Your fathers are no longer alive.]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 1:5
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Zechariah 1:5 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_h3us)

וְ⁠הַ֨⁠נְּבִאִ֔ים הַ⁠לְ⁠עוֹלָ֖ם יִֽחְיֽוּ

**"And the prophets, do they live forever"**

Yahweh is using the question form to emphasize that the prophets also died. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[And the prophets did not live forever either.]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 1:5
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Zechariah 1:6 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_c5ci)

**"But my words and my statutes"**

Yahweh uses the word **But** here to indicate a contrast between the mortality of the fathers and prophets and the enduring effect of God’s words and statutes. In your translation, indicate this contrast in a way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[However, my words and my statutes]

**"did they not overtake your fathers"**

Yahweh is using the question form to emphasize that his words certainly did overtake the ancestors. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. ## Zechariah 1:6 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_xt7o)

דְּבָרַ֣⁠י וְ⁠חֻקַּ֗⁠י \& הֲ⁠ל֥וֹא הִשִּׂ֖יגוּ אֲבֹתֵי⁠כֶ֑ם

**"my words and my statutes \& did they not overtake your fathers"**

Yahweh is speaking of **my words and my statutes** as if they were living things that could pursue and catch people. If it would be helpful in your language, you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[my words and my statutes, … did they not come true for your fathers]

## Zechariah 1:7 (#5) (id: en_tn_ZEC_u9kj)

הַ⁠נָּבִ֖יא לֵ⁠אמֹֽר

**"the prophet, saying"**

The word **saying** normally introduces something Yahweh said. Here it introduces what Zechariah saw in a vision, which he describes in his own words starting in the next verse. If it would be unnatural in your language to use a speech word to introduce a vision, you could use a phrase that introduces a report or account instead, and you should not mark the vision as a quotation. Alternate translation: \[the prophet. This is what Yahweh showed him:] or \[the prophet, and this is what happened:]

See: Connecting Words and Phrases

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 1:7
* **Associated Articles:** Connecting Words and Phrases (ID: `33335@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Berechiah (ID: `person:Berechiah.6`); Iddo (ID: `person:Iddo.6`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Zechariah (ID: `person:Zechariah.17`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`); Darius (ID: `person:Darius.2`)

## Zechariah 1:8 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_g6xm)

רָאִ֣יתִי ׀ הַ⁠לַּ֗יְלָה וְ⁠הִנֵּה

**"I saw in the night, and behold"**

Zechariah is using the phrase **I saw in the night, and behold** to introduce a new event in the story. Use a word, phrase, or other method in your language that is natural for introducing a new event.

וְ⁠הִנֵּה

**"and behold"**

וַ⁠יַּ֗עַן \& וַ⁠יֹּאמַ֑ר

**"answered and said"**

The terms **answered and said** together introduce a direct quotation. In your translation, use a natural way of introducing direct quotations in your language. It may not be necessary to represent both terms explicitly.

## Zechariah 1:12 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_wiu2)

עַד־מָתַ֗י אַתָּה֙ לֹֽא־תְרַחֵ֣ם אֶת־יְרוּשָׁלִַ֔ם וְ⁠אֵ֖ת עָרֵ֣י יְהוּדָ֑ה אֲשֶׁ֣ר זָעַ֔מְתָּה זֶ֖ה שִׁבְעִ֥ים שָׁנָֽה

**"until when \& not have compassion on Jerusalem and on the cities of Judah \& these"**

The angel of Yahweh is using the question form to urgently plead with God to show mercy. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[Please have compassion on Jerusalem and the cities of Judah with which you have been indignant these 70 years now!]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 1:12
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 1:15 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_kzc5)

## Zechariah 1:15 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_rzoy)

אֲשֶׁ֤ר אֲנִי֙ קָצַ֣פְתִּי מְּעָ֔ט וְ⁠הֵ֖מָּה עָזְר֥וּ לְ⁠רָעָֽה

**"for I, I was angry {only} a little, but they helped toward the evil"**

Yahweh uses the word **but** here to indicate a contrast between the limited extent of his anger and the excessive harm the nations inflicted. If your readers would not be familiar with what a measuring line is, you could use the name of a similar thing that your readers would recognize, or you could use a more general term. ## Zechariah 1:17 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_vr1k)

קְרָ֣א לֵ⁠אמֹ֗ר

**"call out, saying"**

There are three levels of quotation here. The angel of Yahweh is speaking to Zechariah (level 1\) and telling him to proclaim a message (level 2\), which itself quotes what Yahweh of Armies says (level 3\). Use the appropriate punctuation in your language to mark these levels of quotation. Alternatively, you could translate this so that there is not a third\-level quotation.

See: Quote Markings

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 1:17
* **Associated Articles:** Quote Markings (ID: `33306@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`)

## Zechariah 1:21 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_bbtm)

אֵ֥לֶּה \& אֵ֣לֶּה הַ⁠קְּרָנ֞וֹת \& וַ⁠יָּבֹ֤אוּ אֵ֨לֶּה֙

**"are} these \& These {are} the horns \& And these have come"**

The word **These** is used three times in this verse with two different referents. The first and third **these** refers to the craftsmen. The second **these** refers to the horns, that is, the powerful nations. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[are these craftsmen … These horns are the nations … And these craftsmen have come]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

כְּ⁠פִי

**"according to the mouth of"**

The angel is using **according to the mouth of** as a common expression of the culture to mean “so that.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[to the extent that]

See: Idiom

## Zechariah 1:21 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_acua)

אִישׁ֙ לֹא־נָשָׂ֣א רֹאשׁ֔⁠וֹ

**"a man {who} has not lifted up his head"**

The angel is using **has not lifted up his head** as a common expression of the culture to mean “no one was able to resist or act with confidence.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[no one was able to resist them] or \[no one dared to oppose them]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 1:21
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Gentiles (ID: `group:Gentile`); Horn (ID: `keyterm:Horn`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`)

## Zechariah 1:21 (#4) (id: en_tn_ZEC_mw4a)

וַ⁠יָּבֹ֤אוּ אֵ֨לֶּה֙ לְ⁠הַחֲרִ֣יד אֹתָ֔⁠ם

**"And these have come to terrify them"**

The term translated as **And** here indicates that what follows is the result or purpose of what came before. The craftsmen have come in order to terrify the nations that scattered Judah. Use a connector in your language that makes this clear. Alternate translation: \[So these have come to terrify them]

See: Connect — Reason\-and\-Result Relationship

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 1:21
* **Associated Articles:** Connect — Reason-and-Result Relationship (ID: `33331@UWTranslationNotes`)

**"the horns of the nations"**

Here, **the horns of the nations** represents the military power of those nations. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[the armies of the nations]

## Zechariah - Chapter 2 Introduction (id: en_tn_ZEC_j9a8)

Zechariah 2 General Notes
=========================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

This chapter is introduced in prose with imagery used in the rest of the chapter to portray Zechariah’s third vision about measuring lines given by the Lord.

Some translations prefer to set apart quotations. The ULT and many other English translations set the lines of 2:4–13, which are extended quotations, farther to the right on the page than the rest of the text.

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### Visions

This book contains a number of visions given to Zechariah, so the images are not ones Zechariah actually saw, but were ones given to him in a vision or dream. It is important for each of these visions to keep their meanings and not be interpreted to mean any specific thing. Please be careful to translate each of the terms in your language.

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 2:0


## Zechariah 2:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_sc5p)

וָ⁠אֶשָּׂ֥א עֵינַ֛⁠י וָ⁠אֵ֖רֶא וְ⁠הִנֵּה

**"And I lifted up my eyes and saw, and behold"**

The phrase **And I lifted up my eyes and saw, and behold** is a way that Zechariah introduces each new vision in his book. Your language may have its own way of introducing a new vision or important event in a narrative.

See: Introduction of a New Event

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 2:1
* **Associated Articles:** Introduction of a New Event (ID: `33419@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Rope (ID: `realia:Rope`)

## Zechariah 2:1 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_jrdm)

וָ⁠אֶשָּׂ֥א עֵינַ֛⁠י

**"And I lifted up my eyes"**

The expression **I lifted up my eyes** was a common expression meaning “I looked up” or “I looked intently.” If this expression does not communicate well in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[I looked up]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 2:1
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Rope (ID: `realia:Rope`)

## Zechariah 2:1 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_bfoo)

וְ⁠הִנֵּה

**"and behold"**

Zechariah is using the term **behold** to focus his listeners’ attention on what he is about to say. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation. Alternate translation: \[and I was surprised to see]

See: Foreground Information

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 2:1

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Rope (ID: `realia:Rope`)

## Zechariah 2:1 (#4) (id: en_tn_ZEC_nzl3)

חֶ֥בֶל מִדָּֽה

**"a rope of measuring"**

Zechariah is using the possessive form to describe a **rope** that is used for **measuring**. If it would be helpful in your language, you could express the idea behind the possessive form with an equivalent phrase. Alternate translation: \[a string for measuring]

See: Possession

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 2:1
* **Associated Articles:** Possession (ID: `33303@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Rope (ID: `realia:Rope`)

## Zechariah - Chapter 3 Introduction (id: en_tn_ZEC_wttt)

Zechariah 3 Introduction
========================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

This chapter presents Zechariah’s fourth night vision: a heavenly courtroom scene in which Joshua the priest stands before the angel of Yahweh while the adversary accuses him (vv.1–5\), followed by a prophetic oracle about “the Branch” and the removal of iniquity (vv.6–10\).

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### Clothing as a Symbol of Moral State

Filthy garments represent sin and defilement, while clean garments and a clean turban represent purification and restored priestly authority.

Translation Issues in This Chapter
----------------------------------

### Nested Quotation Structure

Verses 7–10 contain three levels of quotation: the angel of Yahweh quotes Yahweh of Armies, whose speech includes both a conditional promise and a messianic oracle.

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 3:0

Zechariah is using the word translated as **And** to introduce a new event in the story. Use a word, phrase, or other method in your language that is natural for introducing a new event.

See: Introduction of a New Event

## Zechariah 3:2 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_id6k)

יִגְעַ֨ר יְהוָ֤ה בְּ⁠ךָ֙ הַ⁠שָּׂטָ֔ן וְ⁠יִגְעַ֤ר יְהוָה֙ בְּ⁠ךָ֔ הַ⁠בֹּחֵ֖ר בִּ⁠ירֽוּשָׁלִָ֑ם

## Zechariah 3:2 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_p2h4)

יִגְעַ֨ר יְהוָ֤ה בְּ⁠ךָ֙ הַ⁠שָּׂטָ֔ן וְ⁠יִגְעַ֤ר יְהוָה֙ בְּ⁠ךָ֔ הַ⁠בֹּחֵ֖ר בִּ⁠ירֽוּשָׁלִָ֑ם

**"May Yahweh rebuke you, the adversary, and may Yahweh who is choosing Jerusalem rebuke you"**

Yahweh is expressing what he desires to happen to the adversary. See the discussion in the introduction to this book about this form of speech and follow the decision of your team of how a speaker will express in this context what he wants someone to do. Alternate translation: \[I rebuke you, the adversary, and I, Yahweh who is choosing Jerusalem, rebuke you]

See: Third\-Person Imperatives

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 3:2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Satan (ID: `deity:Satan`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`)

## Zechariah 3:2 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_ysar)

יִגְעַ֨ר יְהוָ֤ה בְּ⁠ךָ֙ הַ⁠שָּׂטָ֔ן וְ⁠יִגְעַ֤ר יְהוָה֙ בְּ⁠ךָ֔ הַ⁠בֹּחֵ֖ר בִּ⁠ירֽוּשָׁלִָ֑ם

**"May Yahweh rebuke you, the adversary, and may Yahweh who is choosing Jerusalem rebuke you"**

These two phrases **May Yahweh rebuke you, the adversary** and **may Yahweh who is choosing Jerusalem rebuke you** mean basically the same thing. The second emphasizes the meaning of the first by repeating the same idea with different words. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could combine them and express the emphasis in another way. Alternate translation: \[Certainly, may Yahweh rebuke you, the adversary]

See: Parallelism

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 3:2
* **Associated Articles:** Parallelism (ID: `33299@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Satan (ID: `deity:Satan`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`)

## Zechariah 3:4 (#4) (id: en_tn_ZEC_p0mg)

הָסִ֛ירוּ הַ⁠בְּגָדִ֥ים הַ⁠צֹּאִ֖ים מֵ⁠עָלָ֑י⁠ו

Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[Take away Joshua’s sin from him]

## Zechariah - Chapter 4 Introduction (id: en_tn_ZEC_hhnv)

Zechariah 4 Introduction
========================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

Zechariah 4 is the fifth of eight night visions. The chapter moves from a prophetic vision (a golden lampstand with seven lamps and two olive trees) to an explanation, then to an oracle declaring that Zerubbabel will complete the temple “not by might and not by power, but by my Spirit,” and concludes with the identification of the two olive trees as those set apart to serve the Lord of all the earth.

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### Temple Furniture as Symbol

The lampstand echoes the tabernacle menorah (Exodus 25:31–40\). Seven lamps and continuous olive oil flowing through golden pipes symbolized God’s provision for continuous worship and the Spirit’s empowering presence.

### Anointing and Divine Empowering

“Sons of the fresh oil” is an idiom for those set apart for special service. Olive oil consecrated kings and priests; here it represents the Spirit enabling Zerubbabel and his community to complete the rebuilding work.

Translation Issues in This Chapter
----------------------------------

### Quotations

Verses 6–10 contain a series of quotes in which it is difficult to discern who is speaking. The ULT suggests:

* the angel speaks from 6–10
* the angel quotes Yahweh in the middle of verse 6 and beginning of verse 9
* the angel prophetically quotes the people’s reaction at the end of verse 7
* the angel, not Zechariah, is the speaker who received the word of Yahweh in verse 8
* the “me” at the end of verse 9 refers to the angel, this is probably the most contested point, scholars are divided as to if this is Zechariah, the angel speaking to him, or the Angel of Yahweh (read as Jesus)

If there is a translation in your area, you may wish to follow its decisions regarding speakers in this section. If not, you may wish to follow the ULT.

### Mountains as Obstacles

Verse 7 calls a great mountain to become a plain, representing political and practical obstacles dissolving so that Zerubbabel can set the capstone in place.

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 4:0


## Zechariah 4:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_kn0c)

וַ⁠יָּ֕שָׁב הַ⁠מַּלְאָ֖ךְ הַ⁠דֹּבֵ֣ר בִּ֑⁠י וַ⁠יְעִירֵ֕⁠נִי

**"And the angel who was speaking with me returned and roused me"**

Zechariah is using the word translated as **returned** to introduce a new event in the story. Use a word, phrase, or other method in your language that is natural for introducing a new event.

See: Introduction of a New Event

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 4:1
* **Associated Articles:** Introduction of a New Event (ID: `33419@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`)

## Zechariah 4:1 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_qlp3)

כְּ⁠אִ֖ישׁ אֲשֶׁר־יֵע֥וֹר מִ⁠שְּׁנָתֽ⁠וֹ

**"like a man who is roused from his sleep"**

The point of this comparison is that just as a man who is roused from his sleep is awakened suddenly, so the angel is rousing Zechariah to pay attention. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[as when a man is suddenly awakened from sleep]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 4:1
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`)

## Zechariah 4:1 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_q632)

אֲשֶׁר־יֵע֥וֹר מִ⁠שְּׁנָתֽ⁠וֹ

**"who is roused from his sleep"**

The expression **is roused from his sleep** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[who someone wakes from sleep]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 4:1
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationNotes`)

**"and its bowl on its top"**

The angel is using the possessive form to describe a **bowl** that belongs to the lampstand. If a speaker of your language would not use the possessive form for this, you could express this idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[and the bowl on top of it]

See: Possession

**"and seven lamps on it, seven and seven tubes"**

## Zechariah 4:5 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_uima)

הֲ⁠ל֥וֹא יָדַ֖עְתָּ מָה־הֵ֣מָּה אֵ֑לֶּה

**"Do you not know what these are"**

The angel is using the question form to express that Zechariah should already know what the items mean. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. וַ֠⁠יַּעַן הַ⁠מַּלְאָ֞ךְ הַ⁠דֹּבֵ֥ר בִּ⁠י֙ וַ⁠יֹּ֣אמֶר

**"And the angel who was speaking with me answered and said"**

The terms **answered and said** together introduce a direct quotation. In your translation, use a natural way of introducing direct quotations in your language. It may not be necessary to represent both terms explicitly.

דְּבַר־יְהוָ֔ה

**"is} the word of Yahweh"**

The angel is using the possessive form to describe a **word** that comes from Yahweh. If a speaker of your language would not use the possessive form for this, you could express this idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[the word from Yahweh]

## Zechariah 4:6 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_tez9)

לֹ֤א בְ⁠חַ֨יִל֙ וְ⁠לֹ֣א בְ⁠כֹ֔חַ כִּ֣י אִם־בְּ⁠רוּחִ֔⁠י

**"Not by might and not by power, but by my Spirit"**

The angel is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[You will not accomplish this by your own might or power, but my Spirit will accomplish it]

* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 4:6 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_jq2a)

אֵלַ⁠י֙ לֵ⁠אמֹ֔ר זֶ֚ה דְּבַר־יְהוָ֔ה אֶל־זְרֻבָּבֶ֖ל לֵ⁠אמֹ֑ר לֹ֤א

**"to me, saying, “This {is} the word of Yahweh to Zerubbabel, saying, ‘Not"**

If it would be clearer in your language, you could translate this so that there is not a quotation within a quotation. Alternate translation: \[to me that this is the message from Yahweh to Zerubbabel: “Not]

* **Associated Articles:** Quotes within Quotes (ID: `33307@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 4:6 (#4) (id: en_tn_ZEC_jbwc)

לֹ֤א בְ⁠חַ֨יִל֙ וְ⁠לֹ֣א בְ⁠כֹ֔חַ

**"Not by might and not by power"**

If your language does not use abstract nouns for the ideas of **might** and **power,** you could express the same ideas in other ways. Alternate translation: \[Not by strength and not by force]

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 4:6
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 4:6 (#5) (id: en_tn_ZEC_udtf)

לֹ֤א בְ⁠חַ֨יִל֙ וְ⁠לֹ֣א בְ⁠כֹ֔חַ

**"Not by might and not by power"**

The terms **might** and **power** mean similar things. The angel is using the two terms together for emphasis. If it would be clearer for your readers, you could express the emphasis with a single phrase. Alternate translation: \[Not by your own strength]

* **Associated Articles:** Doublet (ID: `33266@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 4:7 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_ldqt)

מִֽי־אַתָּ֧ה הַֽר־הַ⁠גָּד֛וֹל לִ⁠פְנֵ֥י זְרֻבָּבֶ֖ל לְ⁠מִישֹׁ֑ר

**"Who {are} you, great mountain, to the face of Zerubbabel? {You} will become a plain"**

The angel is speaking to the great mountain that he knows cannot hear him. He is doing this to communicate to the people listening to him that he feels very strongly about how the obstacles before Zerubbabel will be removed. If it would be helpful in your language, you could translate these words as if the angel were speaking directly to the people about the mountain. Alternate translation: \[Now about this great mountain in front of Zerubbabel, it will become a plain.]

See: Apostrophe

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 4:7
* **Associated Articles:** Apostrophe (ID: `33259@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Zerubbabel (ID: `person:Zerubbabel.2`)

## Zechariah 4:7 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_glle)

מִֽי־אַתָּ֧ה הַֽר־הַ⁠גָּד֛וֹל לִ⁠פְנֵ֥י זְרֻבָּבֶ֖ל לְ⁠מִישֹׁ֑ר

**"Who {are} you, great mountain, to the face of Zerubbabel? {You} will become a plain"**

Here the angel is speaking as if the great mountain were a person standing in the way. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[What are you, great obstacle, before Zerubbabel? You will no longer block him!]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 4:7
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Zerubbabel (ID: `person:Zerubbabel.2`)

## Zechariah 4:7 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_qs31)

מִֽי־אַתָּ֧ה הַֽר־הַ⁠גָּד֛וֹל לִ⁠פְנֵ֥י זְרֻבָּבֶ֖ל

**"Who {are} you, great mountain, to the face of Zerubbabel"**

The angel is using the question form to emphasize that the mountain obstacle will be removed. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[You are nothing, great mountain, before Zerubbabel]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationNotes`)

חֵ֥ן

Zechariah is repeating the word **Grace** in order to intensify his expression of the idea that it conveys. יְדֵ֣י זְרֻבָּבֶ֗ל יִסְּד֛וּ הַ⁠בַּ֥יִת הַ⁠זֶּ֖ה וְ⁠יָדָ֣י⁠ו תְּבַצַּ֑עְנָה

וְ⁠שָׂמְח֗וּ וְ⁠רָא֞וּ

**"And they will rejoice and will see"**

## Zechariah 4:10 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_fgom)

הֵ֥מָּה מְשׁוֹטְטִ֖ים בְּ⁠כָל־הָ⁠אָֽרֶץ

**"they are roaming through all of the earth"**

Yahweh is speaking of the eyes as if they were living things that could roam. If it would be helpful in your language, you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[they look throughout all the earth]

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 4:10
* **Associated Articles:** Personification (ID: `33302@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Zerubbabel (ID: `person:Zerubbabel.2`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Plumb Line (ID: `realia:PlumbLine`)

## Zechariah 4:10 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_y4rs)

כִּ֣י מִ֣י בַז֮ לְ⁠י֣וֹם קְטַנּוֹת֒ וְ⁠שָׂמְח֗וּ

**"For who has despised the day of small things? And they will rejoice"**

The word translated as **For** indicates that what follows is a reason for what came before. Use a connector in your language that makes it clear that what follows is a reason for what came before. Alternate translation: \[Indeed, who would despise the day of small beginnings? It is certain that people will rejoice]

See: Connect — Reason\-and\-Result Relationship

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 4:10
* **Associated Articles:** Connect — Reason-and-Result Relationship (ID: `33331@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Zerubbabel (ID: `person:Zerubbabel.2`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Plumb Line (ID: `realia:PlumbLine`)

## Zechariah 4:10 (#4) (id: en_tn_ZEC_s4lw)

לְ⁠י֣וֹם קְטַנּוֹת֒

**"the day of small things"**

Yahweh is using the possessive form to describe a **day** that is characterized by small things. If a speaker of your language would not use the possessive form for this, you could express this idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[the day when small things happen]

See: Possession

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 4:10
* **Associated Articles:** Possession (ID: `33303@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Zerubbabel (ID: `person:Zerubbabel.2`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Plumb Line (ID: `realia:PlumbLine`)

## Zechariah 4:10 (#5) (id: en_tn_ZEC_hhfv)

מִ֣י בַז֮ לְ⁠י֣וֹם קְטַנּוֹת֒

**"who has despised the day of small things"**

Yahweh is using the question form to emphasize that no one should despise small beginnings. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[no one should despise the day of small things!]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 4:10
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Zerubbabel (ID: `person:Zerubbabel.2`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Plumb Line (ID: `realia:PlumbLine`)

## Zechariah 4:10 (#6) (id: en_tn_ZEC_p47e)

אֶת־הָ⁠אֶ֧בֶן הַ⁠בְּדִ֛יל

**"the stone of tin"**

A **stone of tin** refers to a metal or stone weight tied to a line and hung down from the top of a wall to make sure it is perfectly straight up and down. The common term for this is “plumb line,” as in the UST. If your readers would not be familiar with what a plumb line is, you could use the name of a similar thing that your readers would recognize, or you could use a more general term. Alternate translation: \[a measuring string] or \[a device that proves that the building was constructed properly]

## Zechariah - Chapter 5 Introduction (id: en_tn_ZEC_x1zy)

Zechariah 5 Introduction
========================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

This chapter presents two visions in which an angel interprets what Zechariah sees. The first vision (verses 1–4\) shows a flying scroll carrying a divine curse against thieves and false swearers. The second vision (verses 5–11\) shows evil personified as a woman sealed inside an ephah and carried away to Shinar.

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### Personification of Wickedness

The angel identifies the woman inside the ephah as “the wickedness,” using a human figure to represent the community’s sin. Translators should preserve this identification without reducing it to a mere symbol.

### Ancient Measurements

The chapter refers to cubits (verse 2\) and the ephah (verses 6–10\). These measurement units may need explanatory notes for readers unfamiliar with ancient systems.

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 5:0

## Zechariah 5:2 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_unog)

עֶשְׂרִ֣ים בָּֽ⁠אַמָּ֔ה \& עֶ֥שֶׂר בָּ⁠אַמָּֽה

A **cubit** was a unit of measurement corresponding to the distance between an average person’s elbow and middle fingertip, or about 45 centimeters or 18 inches. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could express the dimensions of the scroll in modern measurements, either in the text or in a footnote. Alternate translation: \[10 meters … 5 meters]

## Zechariah - Chapter 6 Introduction (id: en_tn_ZEC_tfbm)

Zechariah 6 Introduction
========================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

This chapter concludes the eight night visions (vv. 1–8\) with four chariots dispatched as divine agents, then shifts to a sign\-act in which Zechariah crowns Joshua the high priest and delivers the “Branch” oracle (vv. 9–15\).

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### The Branch

The title “Branch” is a messianic designation for a coming king who will build the temple and unite kingship and priesthood on one throne, with “counsel of peace” between the two offices (vv. 12–13\).

Translation Issues in This Chapter
----------------------------------

### Nested Quotations

Verses 10–15 contain three levels of quotation as Yahweh instructs Zechariah to speak to Joshua a message from Yahweh of Armies.

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 6:0


## Zechariah - Chapter 6 Introduction (id: en_tn_ZEC_r5ps)

Zechariah 6 General Notes
=========================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

This chapter is written mainly in prose and tells the last vision of the four chariots in the first part of the chapter.

Some translations set each line of poetry farther to the right than the rest of the text to make it easier to read. The ULT does this with the poetic song in 6:12–13\.

Translation Issues in This Chapter
----------------------------------

### Meaning of the visions

The visions are stated and even when the prophet asked for an explanation, their true meaning is often obscure. This uncertainty should remain in your translation. (See: \[\[rc://\*/tw/dict/bible/kt/prophet]])

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 6:0

## Zechariah 6:8 (#7) (id: en_tn_ZEC_inia)

אֶת־רוּחִ֖⁠י

**"my spirit"**

The pronoun **my** refers to Yahweh, who is speaking through the angel. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[Yahweh’s spirit]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 6:8
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)


## Zechariah 6:9 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_gp4b)

וַ⁠יְהִ֥י דְבַר־יְהוָ֖ה אֵלַ֥⁠י

**"And the word of Yahweh came to me"**

Zechariah is using **the word of Yahweh came to me** as a common expression of the culture to mean “Yahweh communicated a message to me.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[Yahweh communicated a message to me]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 6:9
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`)

## Zechariah 6:9 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_qih7)

וַ⁠יְהִ֥י דְבַר־יְהוָ֖ה אֵלַ֥⁠י לֵ⁠אמֹֽר

**"And the word of Yahweh came to me, saying"**

Zechariah is using the phrase **And the word of Yahweh came to me** to introduce a new event in the story. Use a word, phrase, or other method in your language that is natural for introducing a new event.

See: Introduction of a New Event

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 6:9
* **Associated Articles:** Introduction of a New Event (ID: `33419@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`)

## Zechariah 6:10 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_vhex)

לָק֨וֹחַ֙ מֵ⁠אֵ֣ת הַ⁠גּוֹלָ֔ה

**"Take from the exiles"**

The next verse clarifies that Zechariah is to take silver and gold from the exiles. You could include this information here if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[Take silver and gold from the exiles]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

## Zechariah 6:10 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_mqkj)

הַ⁠גּוֹלָ֔ה

**"the exiles"**

The implication is that **the exiles** refers to people who had returned from exile in Babylon. You could include this information if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[the recently returned exiles]

## Zechariah 6:10 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_wiwz)

מֵ⁠חֶלְדַּ֕י \& טוֹבִיָּ֖ה \& יְדַֽעְיָ֑ה \& יֹאשִׁיָּ֣ה \& צְפַנְיָ֔ה

**"from Heldai \& Tobijah \& Jedaiah \& Josiah \& Zephaniah"**

These are the names of men. This **Josiah** is not the same person as King Josiah.

## Zechariah 6:10 (#4) (id: en_tn_ZEC_tea5)

וּ⁠בָאתָ֤ אַתָּה֙ בַּ⁠יּ֣וֹם הַ⁠ה֔וּא וּ⁠בָ֗אתָ בֵּ֚ית

**"and you, you shall come on that day, and you shall come to the house of"**

If the connection between the phrase **and you, you shall come on that day** and **and you shall come to the house of** is not clear, you may want to use a different connecting word to show how this statement relates to what comes before it. Alternate translation: \[and make sure that you yourself certainly go that very day to the house of]

See: Connecting Words and Phrases

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 6:10
* **Associated Articles:** Connecting Words and Phrases (ID: `33335@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Josiah (ID: `person:Josiah.2`); Tobijah (ID: `person:Tobijah.2`); Jedaiah (ID: `person:Jedaiah.6`); Heldai (ID: `person:Heldai`); Zephaniah (ID: `person:Zephaniah.4`); Babylon (ID: `place:Babylon`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Zechariah 6:10 (#5) (id: en_tn_ZEC_zuuw)

וּ⁠בָאתָ֤ אַתָּה֙

**"and you, you shall come"**

Yahweh is stating the pronoun **you** separately, even though the verb translated as **you shall come** already includes this meaning. He is doing that to emphasize that Zechariah himself is the one who will go to Josiah’s house. If a speaker of your language would use an explicit pronoun for the same purpose, you may want to use that construction in your translation. If not, your language may have other ways of showing the meaning here. Alternate translation: \[you yourself shall come]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 6:10
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Josiah (ID: `person:Josiah.2`); Tobijah (ID: `person:Tobijah.2`); Jedaiah (ID: `person:Jedaiah.6`); Heldai (ID: `person:Heldai`); Zephaniah (ID: `person:Zephaniah.4`); Babylon (ID: `place:Babylon`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Zechariah 6:10 (#6) (id: en_tn_ZEC_imvl)

אֲשֶׁר־בָּ֖אוּ מִ⁠בָּבֶֽל

**"who have come from Babylon"**

The pronoun **who** refers to Heldai, Tobijah, and Jedaiah. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers or restructure the verse to clarify. Alternate translation: \[for Heldai, Tobijah, and Jedaiah have come from Babylon]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 6:11 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_pkx7)

וְ⁠לָקַחְתָּ֥ כֶֽסֶף־וְ⁠זָהָ֖ב וְ⁠עָשִׂ֣יתָ עֲטָר֑וֹת וְ⁠שַׂמְתָּ֗ בְּ⁠רֹ֛אשׁ יְהוֹשֻׁ֥עַ בֶּן־יְהוֹצָדָ֖ק הַ⁠כֹּהֵ֥ן הַ⁠גָּדֽוֹל

**"And you shall take silver and gold, and you shall make crowns, and you shall set {them} on the head of Joshua, the son of Jehozadak, the high priest"**

These instructions are to be carried out in sequence: first take the silver and gold, then make crowns, and then place them on Joshua’s head. Your language may have a connecting phrase that you can use to show this. Alternate translation: \[And you shall take silver and gold, and then you shall make crowns, and then you shall set {them} on the head of Joshua the son of Jehozadak, the high priest.]

See: Connect — Sequential Time Relationship

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 6:11
* **Associated Articles:** Connect — Sequential Time Relationship (ID: `33333@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jehozadak (ID: `person:Jehozadak`); Joshua (ID: `person:Joshua.5`); Crown (ID: `realia:Crown`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`)

## Zechariah 6:11 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_numd)

וְ⁠עָשִׂ֣יתָ עֲטָר֑וֹת וְ⁠שַׂמְתָּ֗ בְּ⁠רֹ֛אשׁ יְהוֹשֻׁ֥עַ

**"and you shall make crowns, and you shall set {them} on the head of Joshua"**

Placing crowns on the head of Joshua the high priest was a symbolic action by which Yahweh showed that a future king\-priest figure would come. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could explain the significance of this action in the text of your translation or in a footnote.

See: Symbolic Action

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 6:11
* **Associated Articles:** Symbolic Action (ID: `33402@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jehozadak (ID: `person:Jehozadak`); Joshua (ID: `person:Joshua.5`); Crown (ID: `realia:Crown`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`)

יְהוֹצָדָ֖ק

**Jehozadak** is the name of a man, the high priest.

## Zechariah 6:12 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_tb6i)

וְ⁠אָמַרְתָּ֤ אֵלָי⁠ו֙ לֵ⁠אמֹ֔ר

**"And you shall speak to him, saying"**

The phrase **And you shall speak to him, saying** introduces a direct quotation. In your translation, use a natural way of introducing direct quotations in your language. It may not be necessary to represent this term explicitly.

See: Quotations and Quote Margins

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 6:12
* **Associated Articles:** Quotations and Quote Margins (ID: `33426@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 6:12 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_lmo7)

לֵ⁠אמֹ֔ר כֹּ֥ה אָמַ֛ר יְהוָ֥ה צְבָא֖וֹת לֵ⁠אמֹ֑ר

**"saying, ‘Thus says Yahweh of Armies, saying"**

The phrase **Yahweh of Armies, saying** introduces a direct quotation within a direct quotation. In your translation, use a natural way of introducing direct quotations in your language. It may not be necessary to represent these terms explicitly.

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 6:12
* **Associated Articles:** Quotations and Quote Margins (ID: `33426@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 6:12 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_sjq0)

כֹּ֥ה אָמַ֛ר יְהוָ֥ה צְבָא֖וֹת לֵ⁠אמֹ֑ר הִנֵּה־אִ֞ישׁ

**"Thus says Yahweh of Armies, saying, Behold, a man"**

If it would be clearer in your language, you could translate this so that there is not a quotation within a quotation. Alternate translation: \[Yahweh of Armies says that there is a man]

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 6:12
* **Associated Articles:** Quotes within Quotes (ID: `33307@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 6:12 (#4) (id: en_tn_ZEC_g376)

הִנֵּה

**"Behold"**

Yahweh is using the term **Behold** to focus his listeners’ attention on what he is about to say. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation. Alternate translation: \[Look]

## Zechariah 6:12 (#5) (id: en_tn_ZEC_mqzw)

צֶ֤מַח שְׁמ⁠וֹ֙

**"Branch is his name"**

Here Yahweh is speaking of this future king as if he were a **Branch** growing from a plant. He means that this person will arise from the line of David. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[Descendant is his name]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 6:12
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

הִנֵּה־אִ֞ישׁ צֶ֤מַח שְׁמ⁠וֹ֙

**"Behold, a man—Branch is his name"**

Here Yahweh is introducing Branch as a new participant in the prophecy. Your language may have its own way of introducing new participants that you can use in your translation.

## Zechariah 6:12 (#7) (id: en_tn_ZEC_f2yp)

צֶ֤מַח \& יִצְמָ֔ח

**"Branch \& he will sprout"**

Yahweh is using a construction in which a verb and its subject come from the same root. You may be able to use the same construction in your language to express the meaning here. Alternatively, your language may have another way of saying this. Alternate translation: \[Branch … he will branch off]

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 6:12

**"and from his place he will sprout"**

Here Yahweh is speaking of this future king as if he were a plant that would **sprout** from the ground. He means that this person will arise and emerge. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and from his place he will arise]

וּ⁠בָנָ֖ה אֶת־הֵיכַ֥ל יְהוָֽהּ

**"and he will build the temple of Yahweh"**

Yahweh is speaking of himself in the third person as **Yahweh**. If it would be helpful in your language, you could translate this in the first person. Alternate translation: \[and he will build my temple]

## Zechariah 6:15 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_g6sg)

וְ⁠הָיָה֙ אִם־שָׁמ֣וֹעַ תִּשְׁמְע֔וּ⁠ן בְּ⁠ק֖וֹל יְהוָ֥ה אֱלֹהֵי⁠כֶֽם

**"And {this} will happen if listening, you will listen to the voice of Yahweh your God"**

Yahweh is presenting a possible condition, that these things will only happen if the people obey Yahweh. Use a natural form in your language for introducing a condition that could happen. It may be helpful to reorder this sentence so that the conditional statement is first. Alternate translation: \[And if you will listen carefully to the voice of Yahweh your God, this will happen]

**"listening, you will listen"**

Yahweh is repeating forms of the verb **listen** in order to intensify his expression of the idea that it conveys. If your language can repeat words for intensification, it would be appropriate to do that here in your translation. If not, your language may have another way of expressing the intensification. ## Zechariah 6:15 (#5) (id: en_tn_ZEC_ke69)

**"you will listen to the voice of Yahweh your God"**

Yahweh is using **listen to the voice of Yahweh your God** as a common expression of the culture to mean “obey what Yahweh your God commands.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. ## Zechariah - Chapter 7 Introduction (id: en_tn_ZEC_x8qj)

Zechariah 7 Introduction
========================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

Zechariah 7 shifts from prophetic visions to prose narrative. A delegation from Bethel asks whether to continue fast in the fifth month, and Yahweh responds with two oracles (vv. 4–7, 8–14\) that redirect the question from ritual observance to justice, covenant faithfulness, and mercy.

### Chapters 7–8 as a Mirrored Unit

Chapters 7–8 are shaped as a mirrored (chiastic) unit: the opening question about fasting (7:1–3\) is answered at the very end (8:18–19\); the first sermon on fasting (7:4–14\) is balanced by a second sermon (8:9–17\), with the covenant sayings (8:1–8\) forming the center.

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### Fasting as Ritual Mourning

The fifth\-month fast commemorated the destruction of Solomon’s temple. Yahweh challenges the people as to whether this practice honored him or merely served the people themselves.

Translation Issues in This Chapter
----------------------------------

### Nested Quotation Levels

Verses 5–7 and 9–10 contain three levels of quotation (narrator, Yahweh, inner message), requiring careful management of quotation marking conventions.

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 7:0

בְּ⁠אַרְבָּעָ֛ה לַ⁠חֹ֥דֶשׁ הַ⁠תְּשִׁעִ֖י

**"on the fourth {day} of the ninth month"**

## Zechariah 7:1 (#5) (id: en_tn_ZEC_vut4)

לַ⁠חֹ֥דֶשׁ הַ⁠תְּשִׁעִ֖י בְּ⁠כִסְלֵֽו

**"of the ninth month, in Kislev"**

The **Kislev** month of the Hebrew calendar includes parts of November and December on Western calendars. You could convert the Hebrew day and month into an approximate date on the calendar that your culture uses. However, the Jews used a lunar calendar, so if you use a solar calendar, the date will be different every year, and the translation will not be entirely accurate. So you may just want to give the number of the day and the name of the month on the Hebrew calendar and say in a footnote approximately what time of year that is on your calendar.

See: Hebrew Months

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 7:1
* **Associated Articles:** Hebrew Months (ID: `33382@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 7:7 (#4) (id: en_tn_ZEC_ylfw)

**"when you still inhabited Jerusalem and the surrounding cities in prosperity"**

Zechariah is using the clause **when you still inhabited Jerusalem and the surrounding cities in prosperity** to describe something that was ongoing. This provides the background for the prophetic messages that Yahweh proclaimed during this time. Connect this clause to the story in a way that makes it clear in your language that this clause is describing something that was ongoing in the background. Alternate translation: \[while you were still living in Jerusalem and the surrounding cities in prosperity and were settled in the Negev, and the foothills to the west]

## Zechariah - Chapter 8 Introduction (id: en_tn_ZEC_joyx)

Zechariah 8 Introduction
========================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

This chapter presents a series of prophetic oracles introduced by the messenger formula “Thus says Yahweh of Armies” moving from Yahweh’s commitment to Zion (vv. 1–8\) through encouragement to rebuild the temple (vv. 9–15\), ethical demands (vv. 16–17\), and a vision of the nations seeking Yahweh (vv. 18–23\).

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### Fasts Becoming Festivals

The four fast days commemorating Jerusalem’s fall will become joyful celebrations when Yahweh restores his people.

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 8:0

כֹּ֚ה אָמַ֣ר יְהוָ֔ה שַׁ֚בְתִּי אֶל־צִיּ֔וֹן

**"Thus says Yahweh, ‘I will return to Zion"**

If it would be clearer in your language, you could translate this so that there is not a quotation within a quotation. In this verse, Zechariah is quoting what Yahweh says directly. Alternate translation: \[Yahweh will return to Zion]

**"and Jerusalem will be called"**

The expression **Jerusalem will be called** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[and people will call Jerusalem]

וְ⁠הַר־יְהוָ֥ה צְבָא֖וֹת

**"and the mountain of Yahweh of Armies"**

Yahweh is speaking of himself in the third person as **Yahweh of Armies**. If it would be helpful in your language, you could translate this in the first person. Alternate translation: \[and my mountain]

## Zechariah 8:3 (#5) (id: en_tn_ZEC_kwrc)

**"and Jerusalem will be called “the city of faithfulness"**

Yahweh is using the possessive form to describe a **city of faithfulness** that is characterized by being faithful. If a speaker of your language would not use the possessive form for this, you could express this idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[the faithful city]

**"and the mountain of Yahweh of Armies"**

**"Old men and old women"**

Yahweh is referring to all elderly people by naming two major components, **Old men** and **old women**. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[The elderly]

## Zechariah 8:4 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_sbnf)

וְ⁠אִ֧ישׁ מִשְׁעַנְתּ֛⁠וֹ בְּ⁠יָד֖⁠וֹ

**"and a man {with} his staff in his hand"**

Here the masculine term **man** has a generic sense that includes both men and women. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could use language in your translation that is clearly inclusive of both men and women. Alternate translation: \[and each person with a staff in hand]

See: When Masculine Words Include Women

Yahweh is using **from a multitude of days** as a common expression of the culture to mean “because of old age.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. וּ⁠רְחֹב֤וֹת הָ⁠עִיר֙ יִמָּ֣לְא֔וּ יְלָדִ֖ים וִֽ⁠ילָד֑וֹת

**"And the open areas of the city will be filled {with} boys and girls"**

## Zechariah 8:6 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_y3bd)

כִּ֣י יִפָּלֵ֗א

**"If it is wonderful"**

Yahweh is speaking as if this were a hypothetical possibility, but he means that it is actually true. If your language does not state something as a condition if it is certain or true, or if your readers might think that what Yahweh is saying is uncertain, then you could translate this as a confident statement. Alternate translation: \[Since it is wonderful]

See: Connect — Factual Conditions

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 8:6
* **Associated Articles:** Connect — Factual Conditions (ID: `33326@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Zechariah 8:6 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_e2td)

בְּ⁠עֵינֵי֙ שְׁאֵרִית֙ הָ⁠עָ֣ם הַ⁠זֶּ֔ה

**"in the eyes of the remnant of this people"**

Yahweh is using **in the eyes of** as a common expression of the culture to mean “in the judgment of” or “in the opinion of.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[in the opinion of the remnant of this people]

## Zechariah 8:6 (#4) (id: en_tn_ZEC_olow)

גַּם \& יִפָּלֵ֔א

**"will it also be wonderful"**

Yahweh is using the question form to emphasize that nothing is too difficult for him. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[it will not be wonderful in my eyes]

נְאֻ֖ם יְהוָ֥ה צְבָאֽוֹת

**"the declaration of Yahweh of Armies"**

The phrase **declaration of Yahweh of Armies** affirms that the preceding words are from Yahweh. In your translation, use a natural way of representing this kind of affirmation in your language.

הִנְ⁠נִ֥י

**"Behold"**

Yahweh is using the term **Behold** to focus his listeners’ attention on what he is about to say. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation. Alternate translation: \[Look!]

See: Foreground Information

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 8:7

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Zechariah 8:7 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_fvja)

מֵ⁠אֶ֣רֶץ מִזְרָ֑ח וּ⁠מֵ⁠אֶ֖רֶץ מְב֥וֹא הַ⁠שָּֽׁמֶשׁ

**"from the land of the sunrise and from the land of the setting of the sun"**

Yahweh is referring to all of the places where his people are scattered by naming two extremes, **the land of the sunrise** and **the land of the setting of the sun**. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[from every country, whether east or west]

See: Merism

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 8:7
* **Associated Articles:** Merism (ID: `33291@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Zechariah 8:8 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_dsbh)

אֶהְיֶ֤ה לָ⁠הֶם֙ לֵֽ⁠אלֹהִ֔ים

**"I will be to them as God"**

Yahweh is using the possessive form to describe a covenant relationship in which he is the God whom the people worship and serve. If a speaker of your language would not use the possessive form for this, you could express this idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[I will be the God whom they worship and serve]

See: Possession

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 8:8
* **Associated Articles:** Possession (ID: `33303@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 8:8 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_k7r2)

וַֽ⁠אֲנִי֙ אֶהְיֶ֤ה

**"and I, I will be"**

Yahweh is stating the pronoun **I** separately, even though the verb translated as **I will be** already includes this meaning. He is doing that to emphasize his own commitment to be the God of his people, matching the reciprocal relationship he describes: they will be his people, and he himself will be their God. If a speaker of your language would use an explicit pronoun for the same purpose, you may want to use that construction in your translation. If not, your language may have other ways of showing the meaning here. Alternate translation: \[and I myself will be]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 8:8
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Truth (ID: `keyterm:Truth`)

## Zechariah 8:8 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_pimv)

בֶּ⁠אֱמֶ֖ת וּ⁠בִ⁠צְדָקָֽה

**"in faithfulness and in righteousness"**

The phrase **in faithfulness and in righteousness** expresses a single idea. The word **faithfulness** tells more about **righteousness**. If it would be more natural in your language, you could express this meaning in a different way. Alternate translation: \[with faithful righteousness]

See: Hendiadys

תֶּחֱזַ֣קְנָה יְדֵי⁠כֶ֔ם

**"May your hands be strong"**

Yahweh is using **hands** to represent the whole person and their courage to work. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression from your culture or state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[Be strong and courageous]

## Zechariah 8:9 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_kc6r)

הַ⁠שֹּֽׁמְעִים֙ בַּ⁠יָּמִ֣ים הָ⁠אֵ֔לֶּה אֵ֖ת הַ⁠דְּבָרִ֣ים הָ⁠אֵ֑לֶּה מִ⁠פִּי֙ הַ⁠נְּבִיאִ֔ים אֲ֠שֶׁר בְּ⁠י֞וֹם יֻסַּ֨ד בֵּית־יְהוָ֧ה צְבָא֛וֹת

**"you who are hearing in these days these words from the mouth of the prophets who {were} there on the day {when} the house of Yahweh of Armies was founded"**

This could mean: (1\) the message Zechariah is giving today is almost the same as the message that Haggai (probably) gave when the temple construction started. Alternate translation: \[you who are hearing today these same words as were from the mouth of the prophets who stood there on the day the house of Yahweh of Armies was founded] (2\) prophets had repeatedly given a similar message ever since temple construction had started, these people had been hearing that. Alternate translation: \[you who have kept hearing in these days these words from the mouth of the prophets ever since the day the house of Yahweh of Armies was founded]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 8:9
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Zechariah 8:10 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_sgqk)

כִּ֗י לִ⁠פְנֵי֙ הַ⁠יָּמִ֣ים הָ⁠הֵ֔ם שְׂכַ֤ר הָֽ⁠אָדָם֙ לֹ֣א נִֽהְיָ֔ה וּ⁠שְׂכַ֥ר הַ⁠בְּהֵמָ֖ה אֵינֶ֑⁠נָּה וְ⁠לַ⁠יּוֹצֵ֨א וְ⁠לַ⁠בָּ֤א אֵין־שָׁלוֹם֙ מִן־הַ⁠צָּ֔ר וַ⁠אֲשַׁלַּ֥ח אֶת־כָּל־הָ⁠אָדָ֖ם אִ֥ישׁ בְּ⁠רֵעֵֽ⁠הוּ

**"For to the face of those days, the wages of man were not, and the wages of the beast, there were none. And for the one going out and for the one coming in there was no peace from the enemy, and I sent every man, each against his neighbor"**

Here Yahweh is providing background information to help his listeners understand the contrast he is making. In your translation, introduce this information in a way that would be natural in your own language and culture.

See: Background Information

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 8:10
* **Associated Articles:** Background Information (ID: `33415@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 8:10 (#4) (id: en_tn_ZEC_bd2h)

שְׂכַ֤ר הָֽ⁠אָדָם֙ לֹ֣א נִֽהְיָ֔ה וּ⁠שְׂכַ֥ר הַ⁠בְּהֵמָ֖ה אֵינֶ֑⁠נָּה

**"the wages of man were not, and the wages of the beast, there were none"**

Yahweh is referring to all sources of income by naming two major components, **the wages of man** and **the wages of the beast**. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[no money could be made in any way]

## Zechariah 8:10 (#5) (id: en_tn_ZEC_gqo9)

וְ⁠לַ⁠יּוֹצֵ֨א וְ⁠לַ⁠בָּ֤א

**"And for the one going out and for the one coming in"**

Yahweh is referring to all of a person’s daily activities by naming two opposites, **the one going out** and **the one coming in**. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[And for everyone in all their activities]

## Zechariah 8:11 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_x8s7)

וְ⁠עַתָּ֗ה לֹ֣א כַ⁠יָּמִ֤ים הָ⁠רִֽאשֹׁנִים֙ אֲנִ֔י לִ⁠שְׁאֵרִ֖ית הָ⁠עָ֣ם הַ⁠זֶּ֑ה נְאֻ֖ם יְהוָ֥ה צְבָאֽוֹת

**"But now, I will not be to the remnant of this people like the former days’—the declaration of Yahweh of Armies"**

Yahweh uses the word **But** here to indicate a contrast between the former days and the present time. In your translation, indicate this contrast in a way that is natural in your language. ## Zechariah 8:12 (#4) (id: en_tn_ZEC_j3pv)

**"the vine will give its fruit, and the land will give its produce, and the heavens will give their dew"**

## Zechariah 8:13 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_zqpi)

וְ⁠הָיָ֡ה כַּ⁠אֲשֶׁר֩ הֱיִיתֶ֨ם קְלָלָ֜ה בַּ⁠גּוֹיִ֗ם בֵּ֤ית יְהוּדָה֙ וּ⁠בֵ֣ית יִשְׂרָאֵ֔ל כֵּ֚ן אוֹשִׁ֣יעַ אֶתְ⁠כֶ֔ם וִ⁠הְיִיתֶ֖ם בְּרָכָ֑ה אַל־תִּירָ֖אוּ תֶּחֱזַ֥קְנָה יְדֵי⁠כֶֽם

**"And it will be just as you were a curse in the nations, house of Judah and house of Israel, so I will save you and you will be a blessing. Do not fear; let your hands be strong"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **you were a curse in the nations** was certainly true, so it will certainly be true that Yahweh will save them and they will be a blessing. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[Just as it was certainly true that you were a curse among the nations, house of Judah and house of Israel, so it will certainly also be true that I save you and you will be a blessing. Do not fear; let your hands be strong!]

See: Connect — Reason\-and\-Result Relationship

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 8:13
* **Associated Articles:** Connect — Reason-and-Result Relationship (ID: `33331@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Zechariah 8:13 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_hzc0)

הֱיִיתֶ֨ם קְלָלָ֜ה בַּ⁠גּוֹיִ֗ם

**"you were a curse in the nations"**

Yahweh is speaking of his people as if they were a **curse**, meaning that other nations would refer to them as an example of what it means to be cursed. Alternate translation: \[the other nations used you as an example of what it means to be cursed]

## Zechariah 8:13 (#4) (id: en_tn_ZEC_hrd4)

בֵּ֤ית יְהוּדָה֙ וּ⁠בֵ֣ית יִשְׂרָאֵ֔ל

**"house of Judah and house of Israel"**

Here, **house** means all the people descended from a particular person. It envisions them as if they were one household living together. So **house of Judah** figuratively means all the people descended from the patriarch Judah, and **house of Israel** means all the people descended from the patriarch Jacob, who was also known as Israel. Alternate translation: \[people of Judah and people of Israel]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 8:13
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 8:23 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_uru3)

**"will grab, and they will grab the hem of"**

To **grab the hem** of someone’s robe was a symbolic action that expressed a strong desire to accompany that person. If this would not be understood in your culture, you could use a comparable symbolic action from your culture or describe what this action meant. ## Zechariah 8:23 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_tvw2)

**"will grab, and they will grab the hem of"**

Yahweh is repeating forms of the verb **will grab** in order to intensify his expression of the idea that it conveys. If your language can repeat words for intensification, it would be appropriate to do that here in your translation. If not, your language may have another way of expressing the intensification. Alternate translation: \[will grab tightly the hem of]

## Zechariah - Chapter 9 Introduction (id: en_tn_ZEC_nhsr)

Zechariah 9 Introduction
========================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

This chapter is a prophetic oracle (the “burden of the word of Yahweh”) that moves from judgment on the surrounding nations (verses 1–8\) to the coming of a righteous, humble king who brings peace (verses 9–10\), then to Yahweh’s deliverance and restoration of his people (verses 11–17\).

### Chapters 9–14 as a Larger Chiasm

Chapters 9–14 are often read as one long, mirrored (chiastic) structure. This chapter’s judgment on the surrounding nations (9:1–8\) is balanced at the very end by Yahweh’s kingship over all the earth (14:16–21\), while themes of celebration, the rejection of false shepherds, and mourning for the pierced one recur in matching pairs in between.

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### The blood of the covenant

In verse 11, “the blood of your covenant” refers to the sacrifices that ratified Yahweh’s agreement with Israel, the basis for his freeing his people.

Translation Issues in This Chapter
----------------------------------

### Verse 1

The first verse has several difficulties. The first set centers on Yahweh’s relationship with Hadrach and Damascus. Many English Bibles translate the preposition here (Yahweh “in” the land) as “against’,” but that preposition does not usually have that meaning. It is also not clear what is resting in Damascus. Is it Yahweh or his word or the burden? What does it mean to be his resting place? For the first set, the text and notes will lean toward an interpretation such as “this prophecy is about some place near Hadrach and Damascus; Yahweh is especially present there right now because he is about to do something.” The second set of difficulties centers on the question of whose eyes are looking at whom? Is Yahweh watching the world and Israelites closely? Are the world and the Israelites watching Yahweh closely (as verse 5 may suggest)? Why (beyond translation)? The text and notes will lean toward the interpretation that Yahweh is watching the world closely. This seems to fit with other parts of the book (4:10, 12:4\) and verse 8\. If a local translation makes a clear decision, you may wish to follow that. Otherwise you may wish to follow the decisions made here.

### Shifting speakers and personified cities

The speaker shifts between the prophet and Yahweh (first person), and cities such as Tyre and Ekron are personified as women; track these shifts throughout the chapter.

### The Lord

In verse 4 and 14, Yahweh is referred to as “Lord.” While we do not recommend following the custom of translating Yahweh as LORD, if that is the decision your translation has made, you will need to take special care in verse 14 where the text reads “the Lord Yahweh” and follow how you have translated that combination in other places (Ezekiel).

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 9:0

**"and heaped up silver like dust, and refined gold like the mud of the streets"**

If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[heaped up silver and refined gold in very great abundance]

וְ⁠עֶקְר֖וֹן כִּֽי־הֹבִ֣ישׁ מֶבָּטָ֑⁠הּ

**"and Ekron, for her hope has dried up"**

Zechariah is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[And Ekron will also see and tremble, for her hope has dried up]

See: Ellipsis

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 9:5
* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); Gaza (ID: `place:Gaza`); Ekron (ID: `place:Ekron`); Ashkelon (ID: `place:Ashkelon`)

## Zechariah 9:5 (#5) (id: en_tn_ZEC_dnjm)

הֹבִ֣ישׁ מֶבָּטָ֑⁠הּ

**"her hope has dried up"**

Here Zechariah is speaking of Ekron’s **hope** as if it were a stream or spring that has **dried up**. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[she has no more hope]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 9:5
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

**"and Ashkelon will not be inhabited"**

The expression **Ashkelon will not be inhabited** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[and people will no longer inhabit Ashkelon]

וְ⁠הִכְרַתִּ֖י גְּא֥וֹן

**"and I will cut off the pride of"**

The pronoun **I** refers to Yahweh. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[and Yahweh will cut off the pride]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 9:6
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 9:6 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_n6lf)

וְ⁠הִכְרַתִּ֖י גְּא֥וֹן פְּלִשְׁתִּֽים

**"and I will cut off the pride of the Philistines"**

Here Yahweh is speaking as if the **pride of the Philistines** were something that he could **cut off** with a blade. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and I will put an end to the arrogance of the Philistines]

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 9:6

גְּא֥וֹן פְּלִשְׁתִּֽים

**"the pride of the Philistines"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **pride**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[what the Philistines are proud of]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 9:6
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Philistines (ID: `group:Philistine`); Ashdod (ID: `place:Ashdod`); Philistia (ID: `place:Philistine`)

## Zechariah 9:6 (#4) (id: en_tn_ZEC_vngh)

וְ⁠הִכְרַתִּ֖י גְּא֥וֹן פְּלִשְׁתִּֽים

**"and I will cut off the pride of the Philistines"**

Here, **the pride of the Philistines** represents their mightiness as seen in the glory of their king and their controlling who lives in their own cities. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[and I will make the Philistines an insignificant people group]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 9:6
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)
