## Zechariah 9:7 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_edfu)

וַ⁠הֲסִרֹתִ֨י דָמָ֜י⁠ו מִ⁠פִּ֗י⁠ו וְ⁠שִׁקֻּצָי⁠ו֙ מִ⁠בֵּ֣ין שִׁנָּ֔י⁠ו

**"And I will remove his blood from his mouth and his abominations from between his teeth"**

Here, **his blood** and **his abominations** represent the meat with blood in it and the food sacrificed to idols that the Philistines ate. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[And I will remove the meat with blood in it from his mouth and the food sacrificed to idols from between his teeth]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 9:7
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Jebusites (ID: `group:Jebusites`); Detested Thing (ID: `keyterm:DetestedThing`); Ekron (ID: `place:Ekron`)

## Zechariah 9:7 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_lcqd)

וַ⁠הֲסִרֹתִ֨י דָמָ֜י⁠ו מִ⁠פִּ֗י⁠ו וְ⁠שִׁקֻּצָי⁠ו֙ מִ⁠בֵּ֣ין שִׁנָּ֔י⁠ו וְ⁠נִשְׁאַ֥ר גַּם־ה֖וּא לֵֽ⁠אלֹהֵ֑י⁠נוּ וְ⁠הָיָה֙ כְּ⁠אַלֻּ֣ף בִּֽ⁠יהוּדָ֔ה

**"And I will remove his blood from his mouth and his abominations from between his teeth, and he also will be left behind for our God, and he will be like a clan in Judah"**

The pronouns **his** and **he** both refer to the Philistines. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[I will remove the Philistines’ blood from their mouth and their abominations from between their teeth, and they also will be left behind for our God, and they will be like a clan in Judah]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 9:7
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Jebusites (ID: `group:Jebusites`); Detested Thing (ID: `keyterm:DetestedThing`); Ekron (ID: `place:Ekron`)

## Zechariah 9:7 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_viil)

וְ⁠הָיָה֙ כְּ⁠אַלֻּ֣ף בִּֽ⁠יהוּדָ֔ה וְ⁠עֶקְר֖וֹן כִּ⁠יבוּסִֽי

**"and he will be like a clan in Judah, and Ekron, like a Jebusite"**

The point of these comparisons is that just as a clan in Judah belongs fully to Yahweh’s people, so the surviving Philistines will belong to Yahweh’s people, and just as a Jebusite was absorbed into Israel, so Ekron will be absorbed among them. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[and the surviving Philistines will belong to Yahweh’s people just as a clan in Judah does, and the people of Ekron will be absorbed among them just as the Jebusites were]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 9:7
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Jebusites (ID: `group:Jebusites`); Detested Thing (ID: `keyterm:DetestedThing`); Ekron (ID: `place:Ekron`)

## Zechariah 9:8 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_id2r)

וְ⁠חָנִ֨יתִי לְ⁠בֵיתִ֤⁠י מִצָּבָה֙ מֵ⁠עֹבֵ֣ר וּ⁠מִ⁠שָּׁ֔ב וְ⁠לֹֽא־יַעֲבֹ֧ר עֲלֵי⁠הֶ֛ם ע֖וֹד נֹגֵ֑שׂ כִּ֥י עַתָּ֖ה רָאִ֥יתִי בְ⁠עֵינָֽ⁠י

**"And I will camp around my house as a guard against the one passing through and against the one returning, and no oppressor will pass over them again, for now I have seen with my eyes"**

If it would be more natural in your language, you could reverse the order of these phrases, since the last phrase gives the reason for the result that the rest of the verse describes. Alternate translation: \[I am paying close attention to what happens to my people like one camping around my house as a guard against the one passing through and against the one returning, and no oppressor will pass over them again.]

See: Connect — Reason\-and\-Result Relationship

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 9:8
* **Associated Articles:** Connect — Reason-and-Result Relationship (ID: `33331@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Zechariah 9:8 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_ik13)

וְ⁠חָנִ֨יתִי לְ⁠בֵיתִ֤⁠י

**"And I will camp around my house"**

Here Yahweh is speaking as if he were an army that would **camp around** his **house** to protect it. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[And I will stand guard around my house]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 9:8
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Zechariah 9:8 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_s0oa)

לְ⁠בֵיתִ֤⁠י

**"around my house"**

Here, **my house** represents his temple and the people connected with it. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[my temple and those who belong to it]

מֵ⁠עֹבֵ֣ר וּ⁠מִ⁠שָּׁ֔ב

**"against the one passing through and against the one returning"**

Yahweh is using the expressions **the one passing through** and **the one returning** as nouns to mean certain kinds of people. Your language may use such expressions in the same way. If not, you can translate them with equivalent phrases. Alternate translation: \[against enemies who pass through and the enemies who return]

עֲלֵי⁠הֶ֛ם

**"them"**

The pronoun **them** refers to the people connected with Yahweh’s house. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[my people]

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 9:8
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Zechariah 9:8 (#6) (id: en_tn_ZEC_ubbc)

עַתָּ֖ה רָאִ֥יתִי בְ⁠עֵינָֽ⁠י

**"now I have seen with my eyes"**

Here, **now I have seen with my eyes** represents “I have begun to pay close attention to and care for my people.” If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[now I have begun to care for my people]

## Zechariah 9:9 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_u6x5)

גִּילִ֨י מְאֹ֜ד בַּת־צִיּ֗וֹן הָרִ֨יעִי֙ בַּ֣ת יְרוּשָׁלִַ֔ם

**"Rejoice greatly, daughter of Zion! Shout, daughter of Jerusalem"**

The two clauses **Rejoice greatly, daughter of Zion** and **Shout, daughter of Jerusalem** mean basically the same thing. The second emphasizes the meaning of the first by repeating the same idea with different words. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could combine them and express the emphasis in another way. Alternate translation: \[Rejoice with great joy, people of Zion and Jerusalem!]

See: Parallelism

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 9:9
* **Associated Articles:** Parallelism (ID: `33299@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Donkey (ID: `fauna:Donkey`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`)

## Zechariah 9:9 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_dypn)

וְ⁠נוֹשָׁ֖ע ה֑וּא

**"and being saved is he"**

The expression **being saved** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[and God saves him]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 9:9
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationNotes`)

בַּת־צִיּ֗וֹן \& בַּ֣ת יְרוּשָׁלִַ֔ם

**"daughter of Zion \& daughter of Jerusalem"**

Here Zechariah is speaking of the people of Jerusalem as if they were a **daughter of Zion** or **daughter of Jerusalem**. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[people of Zion … people of Jerusalem]

הִנֵּ֤ה

**"Behold"**

Zechariah is using the term **Behold** to focus his listeners’ attention on what he is about to say. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation. Alternate translation: \[Look]

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Donkey (ID: `fauna:Donkey`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`)

## Zechariah 9:9 (#5) (id: en_tn_ZEC_etid)

וְ⁠רֹכֵ֣ב עַל־חֲמ֔וֹר וְ⁠עַל־עַ֖יִר בֶּן־אֲתֹנֽוֹת

**"and riding on a donkey, and on a colt, the son of female donkeys"**

These two clauses mean basically the same thing. There are not two donkeys. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could combine them. Alternate translation: \[and riding on a donkey, even on a young donkey] or \[and riding on a donkey’s colt]

עַ֖יִר בֶּן־אֲתֹנֽוֹת

**"a colt, the son of female donkeys"**

It might seem that the expression **a colt, the son of female donkeys** contains extra information that would not be natural to express in your language. If so, you could shorten it. Alternate translation: \[a young donkey]

## Zechariah 9:10 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_vs75)

וְ⁠הִכְרַתִּי־רֶ֣כֶב מֵ⁠אֶפְרַ֗יִם וְ⁠סוּס֙ מִ⁠יר֣וּשָׁלִַ֔ם וְ⁠נִכְרְתָה֙ קֶ֣שֶׁת מִלְחָמָ֔ה

**"And I will cut off the chariot from Ephraim, and the horse from Jerusalem, and the bow of war will be cut off"**

**"the bow of war"**

## Zechariah 9:10 (#5) (id: en_tn_ZEC_cv3r)

וְ⁠דִבֶּ֥ר שָׁל֖וֹם לַ⁠גּוֹיִ֑ם

**"And he will speak peace to the nations"**

Zechariah is using **speak peace** as a common expression of the culture to mean “proclaim peace.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[And he will make peace with the nations] or \[And he will make the nations behave peacefully]

**"And he will speak peace to the nations"**

## Zechariah 9:10 (#8) (id: en_tn_ZEC_hl9c)

מִ⁠יָּ֣ם עַד־יָ֔ם וּ⁠מִ⁠נָּהָ֖ר עַד־אַפְסֵי־אָֽרֶץ

**"will be from sea to sea, and from the River to the ends of the earth"**

Yahweh is referring to all of the earth by naming two major regions, **from sea to sea, and from the River to the ends of the earth**. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[across the entire earth]

See: Merism

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 9:10
* **Associated Articles:** Merism (ID: `33291@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 9:11 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_cfry)

גַּם־אַ֣תְּ

**"As for} you also"**

Here Zechariah is using the phrase **As for you also** to introduce the people of Jerusalem as the ones Yahweh now addresses directly. Your language may have its own way of introducing new participants that you can use in your translation. Alternate translation: \[As for you also, O Jerusalem]

See: Introduction of New and Old Participants

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 9:11
* **Associated Articles:** Introduction of New and Old Participants (ID: `33421@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 9:11 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_yw5g)

בְּ⁠דַם־בְּרִיתֵ֗⁠ךְ

**"by the blood of your covenant"**

Here, **the blood of your covenant** represents the sacrificial blood by which Yahweh confirmed his covenant with the people. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[by the sacrificial blood that sealed my covenant with you]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 9:11
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationNotes`)

שִׁלַּ֤חְתִּי אֲסִירַ֨יִ⁠ךְ֙

**"I have set free your prisoners"**

The pronoun **I** refers to Yahweh. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[Yahweh has set free your prisoners]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 9:11
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 9:11 (#4) (id: en_tn_ZEC_k51o)

אֲסִירַ֨יִ⁠ךְ֙ מִ⁠בּ֔וֹר אֵ֥ין מַ֖יִם בּֽ⁠וֹ

**"your prisoners from the pit in which there is no water"**

Here Yahweh is speaking of the exile and suffering of the people as if they were **prisoners** held in a **pit in which there is no water**. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[your captives who are dying in desperate misery]

* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

אֲסִירֵ֖י הַ⁠תִּקְוָ֑ה

**"prisoners of hope"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **hope**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[prisoners who are hopeful]

* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 9:12 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_mxos)

שׁ֚וּבוּ לְ⁠בִצָּר֔וֹן אֲסִירֵ֖י הַ⁠תִּקְוָ֑ה

**"Return to the stronghold, prisoners of hope"**

Here Yahweh is speaking of a place of safety and security as if it were a **stronghold** to which the people could return. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[Return to the place where you will be safe, prisoners of hope]

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 9:12
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 9:12 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_kdep)

מִשְׁנֶ֖ה אָשִׁ֥יב לָֽ⁠ךְ

**"that I will return double to you"**

Yahweh is using **return double to you** as a common expression of the culture to mean “restore to you twice as much as you have lost.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[that I will restore to you twice as much as you have lost]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 9:12
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

כִּֽי

**"your sons, Zion, against your sons, Greece"**

Yahweh is using **your sons, Zion** and **your sons, Greece** as common expressions of the culture to mean the people of Zion and the people of Greece. If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. ## Zechariah 9:13 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_r0is)

דָרַ֨כְתִּי לִ֜⁠י יְהוּדָ֗ה קֶ֚שֶׁת מִלֵּ֣אתִי אֶפְרַ֔יִם

**"I have bent for myself Judah, a bow; I have filled Ephraim"**

Here Yahweh is speaking of the people of **Judah** as if they were a **bow** that he bends and of **Ephraim** as the arrow that he uses. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[I have made the people of Judah like a bow for myself, and I have made the people of Ephraim like arrows]

## Zechariah 9:13 (#4) (id: en_tn_ZEC_gcsj)

מִלֵּ֣אתִי אֶפְרַ֔יִם

**"I have filled Ephraim"**

Yahweh is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[I have filled Ephraim as a quiver of arrows]

**"and I will make you like the sword of a mighty man"**

## Zechariah - Chapter 10 Introduction (id: en_tn_ZEC_sbwk)

Zechariah 10 Introduction
=========================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

This prophetic poem first contrasts Yahweh, who gives rain and guidance, with worthless idols and diviners (verses 1–2\), then becomes a first\-person divine promise to strengthen and regather his scattered people (verses 3–12\).

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### Shepherds and leaders

The shepherds and male goats represent the people’s leaders, while Yahweh is the true shepherd who cares for his flock.

### Regathering the exiles

Yahweh promises to bring the house of Judah and the house of Joseph (Ephraim, the northern kingdom) back from Egypt, Assyria, and distant lands.

Translation Issues in This Chapter
----------------------------------

### Shifts in speaker

The chapter alternates between describing Yahweh and Yahweh speaking as “I,” and in verse 11 the referent of “he” is ambiguous. Keep these referents clear.

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 10:0

Zechariah is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[to a man he gives vegetation in the field]

See: Ellipsis

## Zechariah 10:3 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_hryy)

עַל־הָֽ⁠רֹעִים֙ חָרָ֣ה אַפִּ֔⁠י וְ⁠עַל־הָ⁠עַתּוּדִ֖ים אֶפְק֑וֹד

**"Against the shepherds my nose burns, and on the male goats I will attend"**

These two clauses mean basically the same thing. The second emphasizes the meaning of the first by repeating the same idea with different words. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could combine them and express the emphasis in another way. Alternate translation: \[I am furious with the shepherds who lead my people, and I will certainly deal with those leaders]

See: Parallelism

**"the house of Judah \& the house of Joseph"**

You could indicate this explicitly in your translation if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[Yahweh will clear away every danger that threatens his people, just as he dried up the sea and the Jordan for their ancestors]

## Zechariah - Chapter 11 Introduction (id: en_tn_ZEC_krd3)

Zechariah 11 Introduction
=========================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

The chapter opens with a poetic taunt of judgment (verses 1–3\), moves to a prophetic sign\-act in which Zechariah acts as a shepherd (verses 4–14\), and closes with a woe oracle against a worthless shepherd (verses 15–17\). The ULT sets verses 1–3 and 17 as poetry.

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### Shepherds and the flock

Throughout the chapter, the shepherd represents a ruler, and the flock represents the people he leads.

### The two staffs

Zechariah names two shepherd’s staffs “Favor” and “Union;” breaking them signifies the ending of Yahweh’s covenant and of the unity between Judah and Israel.

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 11:0


## Zechariah 11:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_rv7o)

פְּתַ֥ח לְבָנ֖וֹן דְּלָתֶ֑י⁠ךָ

**"Open your doors, Lebanon"**

Zechariah is speaking to **Lebanon**, a place that he knows cannot hear him. He is doing this to communicate to the people listening to him that he feels very strongly about the coming destruction of the land. If it would be helpful in your language, you could translate these words as if he were speaking directly to the people about Lebanon. Alternate translation: \[People of Lebanon, open your doors]

See: Apostrophe

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 11:1
* **Associated Articles:** Apostrophe (ID: `33259@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cedar (ID: `flora:Cedar`); Lebanon (ID: `place:Lebanon`)

## Zechariah 11:1 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_bh82)

וְ⁠תֹאכַ֥ל אֵ֖שׁ בַּ⁠אֲרָזֶֽי⁠ךָ

**"that fire may devour among your cedars"**

Zechariah is speaking of **fire** as if it were a living thing that could **devour** the trees. If it would be helpful in your language, you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[that fire may burn up your cedars]

See: Personification

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 11:1
* **Associated Articles:** Personification (ID: `33302@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cedar (ID: `flora:Cedar`); Lebanon (ID: `place:Lebanon`)

## Zechariah 11:2 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_pxdt)

הֵילֵ֤ל \& הֵילִ֨ילוּ֙

**"Wail \& Wail"**

The word translated as **wail** copies the sound of crying or howling. Zechariah uses this word here, and a similar word about lions in verse 3, to make the scene sound sad and urgent, like people weeping and lions roaring. If your language has a word that sounds like crying, use it here. If not, choose words that still sound emotional and urgent, not calm or formal.

See: Poetry

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 11:2
* **Associated Articles:** Poetry (ID: `33422@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Fir (ID: `flora:Fir`); Bashan (ID: `place:Bashan`); Mighty (ID: `keyterm:Mighty`); Cedar (ID: `flora:Cedar`); Grecian Juniper (ID: `flora:Juniper`); Sandalwood (ID: `flora:Sandalwood`); Cypress (ID: `flora:Cypress`); Oak (ID: `flora:Oak`)

## Zechariah 11:2 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_rp6k)

בְּרוֹשׁ֙

**"cypress"**

The word **cypress** refers to a tall evergreen tree, but its exact identification is uncertain; other translations render it as “fir,” “pine,” or “juniper.” If your readers would not be familiar with this kind of tree, you could use the name of a similar tall, valuable evergreen tree that they would recognize, or you could use a more general term, such as “large evergreen tree.” Alternate translation: \[fir tree] or \[pine tree]

See: Translate Unknowns

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 11:2
* **Associated Articles:** Translate Unknowns (ID: `33408@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 11:2 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_vbws)

הֵילֵ֤ל בְּרוֹשׁ֙

**"Wail, cypress"**

Here Zechariah is speaking of the cypress trees as if they were a person who could **wail**. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[Cry out, cypress, like a mourner] or \[Cypress, be greatly distressed]

## Zechariah 11:2 (#4) (id: en_tn_ZEC_jdel)

הֵילֵ֤ל בְּרוֹשׁ֙ כִּֽי־נָ֣פַל אֶ֔רֶז אֲשֶׁ֥ר אַדִּרִ֖ים שֻׁדָּ֑דוּ הֵילִ֨ילוּ֙ אַלּוֹנֵ֣י בָשָׁ֔ן כִּ֥י יָרַ֖ד יַ֥עַר ה⁠בצור

**"Wail, cypress, for the cedar has fallen, because the majestic ones have been devastated! Wail, oaks of Bashan, for the fortified forest has gone down"**

The word translated as **for** indicates that what follows is a reason for what came before. Use a connector in your language that makes it clear that what follows is a reason for what came before. Alternate translation: \[Because the cedar has fallen, wail, cypress, since the majestic ones have been devastated! Because the fortified forest has gone down, wail, oaks of Bashan]

See: Connect — Reason\-and\-Result Relationship

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 11:2
* **Associated Articles:** Connect — Reason-and-Result Relationship (ID: `33331@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Fir (ID: `flora:Fir`); Bashan (ID: `place:Bashan`); Mighty (ID: `keyterm:Mighty`); Cedar (ID: `flora:Cedar`); Grecian Juniper (ID: `flora:Juniper`); Sandalwood (ID: `flora:Sandalwood`); Cypress (ID: `flora:Cypress`); Oak (ID: `flora:Oak`)

## Zechariah 11:2 (#5) (id: en_tn_ZEC_w36e)

נָ֣פַל אֶ֔רֶז

**"the cedar has fallen"**

Zechariah is speaking of powerful rulers as if they were a **cedar** tree that had fallen. A cedar was one of the tallest, strongest trees in that area, so a fallen cedar pictures a powerful ruler who has lost his power. The text does not say exactly which ruler or nation this is, so keep your translation general instead of naming a specific person or place. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[the powerful rulers have been overthrown]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 11:2
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Fir (ID: `flora:Fir`); Bashan (ID: `place:Bashan`); Mighty (ID: `keyterm:Mighty`); Cedar (ID: `flora:Cedar`); Grecian Juniper (ID: `flora:Juniper`); Sandalwood (ID: `flora:Sandalwood`); Cypress (ID: `flora:Cypress`); Oak (ID: `flora:Oak`)

## Zechariah 11:2 (#6) (id: en_tn_ZEC_u0c3)

אַדִּרִ֖ים שֻׁדָּ֑דוּ

**"the majestic ones have been devastated"**

The expression **have been devastated** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[enemies have devastated the majestic ones]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 11:2
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Fir (ID: `flora:Fir`); Bashan (ID: `place:Bashan`); Mighty (ID: `keyterm:Mighty`); Cedar (ID: `flora:Cedar`); Grecian Juniper (ID: `flora:Juniper`); Sandalwood (ID: `flora:Sandalwood`); Cypress (ID: `flora:Cypress`); Oak (ID: `flora:Oak`)

## Zechariah 11:2 (#7) (id: en_tn_ZEC_bz7j)

יַ֥עַר ה⁠בצור

**"the fortified forest"**

The word translated as **fortified** has a similar spelling to the word for **vintage/grape harvest**. Some ancient manuscripts and versions read this as “the forest of the vintage,” apparently confusing the two similar words. The ULT and UST follow the reading “the fortified forest” (i.e., an inaccessible or lofty forest), which matches the word form actually present here. If a translation of the Bible exists in your region, you may wish to use the reading that it uses. If a translation of the Bible does not exist in your region, you may wish to use the reading of the ULT. Alternate translation: \[the forest of the vintage has come down]

See: Textual Variants

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 11:2
* **Associated Articles:** Textual Variants (ID: `33405@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Fir (ID: `flora:Fir`); Bashan (ID: `place:Bashan`); Mighty (ID: `keyterm:Mighty`); Cedar (ID: `flora:Cedar`); Grecian Juniper (ID: `flora:Juniper`); Sandalwood (ID: `flora:Sandalwood`); Cypress (ID: `flora:Cypress`); Oak (ID: `flora:Oak`)

## Zechariah 11:3 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_h9cj)

ק֚וֹל יִֽלְלַ֣ת הָ⁠רֹעִ֔ים כִּ֥י שֻׁדְּדָ֖ה אַדַּרְתָּ֑⁠ם ק֚וֹל שַׁאֲגַ֣ת כְּפִירִ֔ים כִּ֥י שֻׁדַּ֖ד גְּא֥וֹן הַ⁠יַּרְדֵּֽן

**"The voice of the wailing of the shepherds, for their glory is devastated! The voice of the roaring of the young lions, for the pride of the Jordan is devastated"**

These two clauses mean basically the same thing. The second emphasizes the meaning of the first by repeating the same idea with different words. The word for glory here is a lot like the word for the “majestic ones” in verse 2\. Zechariah repeats this idea to show that everything great and important, both the leaders and the land, has been completely ruined. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could combine them and express the emphasis in another way. Alternate translation: \[Listen to the shepherds wailing and the young lions roaring, for everything of value to them has been utterly devastated]

See: Parallelism

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 11:3
* **Associated Articles:** Parallelism (ID: `33299@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jordan River (ID: `place:Jordan`); Lion (ID: `fauna:Lion`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`)

## Zechariah 11:3 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_owd1)

יִֽלְלַ֣ת הָ⁠רֹעִ֔ים \& שַׁאֲגַ֣ת כְּפִירִ֔ים

**"the wailing of the shepherds \& the roaring of the young lions"**

Zechariah is speaking of the rulers of the land as if they were **shepherds** and **young lions** that were wailing and roaring over what they had lost. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[the crying of the rulers … the mourning of the powerful men]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 11:3 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_ot39)

גְּא֥וֹן הַ⁠יַּרְדֵּֽן

**"the pride of the Jordan"**

Zechariah is using **the pride of the Jordan** as a common expression of the culture to mean the dense thickets of trees and bushes that grow along the Jordan River. If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[the dense thickets along the Jordan River are devastated]

אֲשֶׁ֨ר קֹנֵי⁠הֶ֤ן יַֽהֲרְגֻ⁠ן֙ וְ⁠לֹ֣א יֶאְשָׁ֔מוּ וּ⁠מֹכְרֵי⁠הֶ֣ן יֹאמַ֔ר

נְאֻם־יְהוָ֑ה

## Zechariah 11:6 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_zfze)

אָנֹכִ֜י מַמְצִ֣יא אֶת־הָ⁠אָדָ֗ם אִ֤ישׁ בְּ⁠יַד־רֵעֵ֨⁠הוּ֙ וּ⁠בְ⁠יַ֣ד מַלְכּ֔⁠וֹ

**"I am causing mankind to fall, each man, in the hand of his neighbor and in the hand of his king"**

Here Yahweh is speaking as if he were causing people to **fall** into the power of their neighbors and king, as though pushing them down so that others could seize them. If it would be helpful in your language, you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[I am handing mankind over, each man, to his neighbor and to his king]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 11:6
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Zechariah 11:6 (#4) (id: en_tn_ZEC_hpif)

אֶת־הָ⁠אָדָ֗ם

**"mankind"**

Here the masculine terms **mankind** and **each man** have a generic sense that includes both men and women. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could use language in your translation that is clearly inclusive of both men and women. Alternate translation: \[all people]

See: When Masculine Words Include Women

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 11:6

## Zechariah 11:6 (#5) (id: en_tn_ZEC_m1yq)

בְּ⁠יַד־רֵעֵ֨⁠הוּ֙ וּ⁠בְ⁠יַ֣ד מַלְכּ֔⁠וֹ \& מִ⁠יָּדָֽ⁠ם

**"in the hand of his neighbor and in the hand of his king \& from their hand"**

Here, **hand** represents power or control. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[under the control of his neighbor and of his king … from their power]

עֲנִיֵּ֣י הַ⁠צֹּ֑אן

Zechariah is using the adjective **poor** as a noun to mean a certain kind of sheep. Your language may use adjectives in the same way. If not, you can translate this adjective with an equivalent phrase. Alternate translation: \[the suffering sheep of the flock]

## Zechariah 11:7 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_wqe4)

לְ⁠אַחַ֞ד קָרָ֤אתִי נֹ֨עַם֙ וּ⁠לְ⁠אַחַד֙ קָרָ֣אתִי חֹֽבְלִ֔ים

**"For the one I called “Favor,” and for the other I called “Union"**

The words **Favor** and **Union** are names that Zechariah gave to his two shepherd’s staffs. He chose names that carry meaning, since the staffs represent Yahweh’s relationship with the people.

## Zechariah 11:8 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_svqw)

אֶת־שְׁלֹ֥שֶׁת הָ⁠רֹעִ֖ים

**"the three shepherds"**

The implication is that these **three shepherds** were leaders of the people whom Yahweh removed from their position. You could include this information if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[the three leaders who were poorly shepherding the people]

* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 11:8 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_s5d9)

וַ⁠תִּקְצַ֤ר נַפְשִׁ⁠י֙ בָּ⁠הֶ֔ם

**"And my soul became short with them"**

Zechariah is using **my soul became short with them** as a common expression of the culture to mean that he became impatient with them. If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[And I became tired of them]

## Zechariah 11:9 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_f4fh)

הַ⁠מֵּתָ֣ה תָמ֗וּת וְ⁠הַ⁠נִּכְחֶ֨דֶת֙ תִּכָּחֵ֔ד

**"The one dying, let it die, and the one being destroyed, let it be destroyed"**

Zechariah is repeating forms of the verbs **die** and **destroy** in order to intensify his expression of the idea that the flock is doomed and beyond saving. If your language can repeat words for intensification, it would be appropriate to do that here in your translation. If not, your language may have another way of expressing the intensification. Alternate translation: \[Let the ones who are going to die, die, and let the ones who are going to be destroyed, be destroyed]

See: Reduplication

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 11:9

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`)

## Zechariah 11:9 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_o0py)

וְ⁠הַ⁠נִּכְחֶ֨דֶת֙ תִּכָּחֵ֔ד

**"and the one being destroyed, let it be destroyed"**

The expression **let it be destroyed** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[and let the one perishing perish]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 11:9
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 11:9 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_uhdn)

תֹּאכַ֕לְנָה אִשָּׁ֖ה אֶת־בְּשַׂ֥ר רְעוּתָֽ⁠הּ

**"let them eat, each one, the flesh of her companion"**

Zechariah is speaking of the people as if they were sheep that would eat the flesh of one another. He means mean that they will turn against and destroy one another. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[let each one destroy the other]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 11:9
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`)

## Zechariah 11:10 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_wdq5)

וָ⁠אֶקַּ֤ח אֶת־מַקְלִ⁠י֙ אֶת־נֹ֔עַם וָ⁠אֶגְדַּ֖ע אֹת֑⁠וֹ לְ⁠הָפֵיר֙ אֶת־בְּרִיתִ֔⁠י אֲשֶׁ֥ר כָּרַ֖תִּי אֶת־כָּל־הָ⁠עַמִּֽים

**"And I took my staff Favor, and I chopped it up, to break my covenant that I had cut with all the peoples"**

Here, **to** marks the words **break my covenant that I had cut with all the peoples** as the goal or purpose of his chopping up the **staff Favor**. Use a connector in your language that makes it clear that this is the purpose.

See: Connect — Goal (Purpose) Relationship

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 11:10
* **Associated Articles:** Connect — Goal (Purpose) Relationship (ID: `33330@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Covenant (ID: `keyterm:Covenant`); Staff (ID: `realia:Staff`); Rod (ID: `realia:Rod`)

## Zechariah 11:10 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_gzwn)

אֶת־בְּרִיתִ֔⁠י אֲשֶׁ֥ר כָּרַ֖תִּי

**"my covenant that I had cut"**

Zechariah is using the word **cut** as a common expression of the culture to mean “make,” since in that culture people spoke of making a covenant as cutting it. If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[my covenant that I had made]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 11:10
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Covenant (ID: `keyterm:Covenant`); Staff (ID: `realia:Staff`); Rod (ID: `realia:Rod`)

## Zechariah 11:10 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_ykzw)

כָּל־הָ⁠עַמִּֽים

**"all the peoples"**

This could mean: (1\) all the nations of the world. Alternate translation: \[all the world’s nations] (2\) the Israelite nation. Alternate translation: \[all of the Israelites]

וַ⁠תֻּפַ֖ר

**"And it was broken"**

## Zechariah 11:11 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_nx3e)

וַ⁠תֻּפַ֖ר

**"And it was broken"**

The pronoun **it** refers to the covenant that Yahweh had made with all the peoples, mentioned in the previous verse. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[And the covenant was broken]

## Zechariah 11:12 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_wj8h)

אִם־ט֧וֹב בְּ⁠עֵינֵי⁠כֶ֛ם הָב֥וּ שְׂכָרִ֖⁠י וְ⁠אִם־לֹ֣א ׀ חֲדָ֑לוּ

**"If it is good in your eyes, give my wages; but if not, refrain"**

Zechariah is presenting a possible condition, that the people may or may not think it is good to give him his wages. Use a natural form in your language for introducing a condition that could happen. Alternate translation: \[If it is good in your eyes, then give my wages; but if it is not good, then refrain]

See: Connect — Hypothetical Conditions

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 11:12
* **Associated Articles:** Connect — Hypothetical Conditions (ID: `33327@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Money (ID: `realia:Money`)

## Zechariah 11:12 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_vods)

אִם־ט֧וֹב בְּ⁠עֵינֵי⁠כֶ֛ם

**"If it is good in your eyes"**

Zechariah is using **good in your eyes** as a common expression of the culture to mean that something seems good or right to a person. If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[If you consider it good]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 11:12
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Money (ID: `realia:Money`)

## Zechariah 11:12 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_joqs)

שְׁלֹשִׁ֥ים כָּֽסֶף

**"thirty {pieces} of silver"**

In ancient times, a piece of silver was a coin or weight of silver called a shekel, which weighed about 11 grams, or about a third of an ounce. You could try to express this in terms of modern money values, but if you did, that could cause your Bible translation to become outdated and inaccurate, since those values can change over time. Instead, you might say something general like “thirty silver coins,” or give the equivalent weight, or use the term in the text and give the weight in a note.

See: Biblical Money

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 11:12
* **Associated Articles:** Biblical Money (ID: `33372@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Money (ID: `realia:Money`)

## Zechariah 11:13 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_asad)

הַ⁠יּוֹצֵ֔ר \& הַ⁠יּוֹצֵֽר

**"the potter \& the potter"**

A **potter** is a kind of craftsman who forms objects out of clay. If your readers would not be familiar with what a potter is, you could use the name of a similar thing that your readers would recognize, or you could use a more general term. Alternate translation: \[the craftsman who makes things out of clay … the craftsman who makes things out of clay]

See: Translate Unknowns

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 11:13
* **Associated Articles:** Translate Unknowns (ID: `33408@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); House (ID: `realia:House`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`)

## Zechariah 11:13 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_a5iz)

אֶ֣דֶר הַ⁠יְקָ֔ר אֲשֶׁ֥ר יָקַ֖רְתִּי

**"the magnificence of the price at which I was valued"**

Yahweh actually means to communicate the opposite of the literal meaning of what he is saying here, since thirty pieces of silver was in fact a contemptibly small price. It was also the amount the owner of an ox that gored a slave to death had to give to the owner of the slave (see Exo 21:32\), so by paying that amount they were saying he was worth the same as a dead slave. If a speaker of your language would not do that, you could translate the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[the contemptibly small price at which I was valued]

See: Irony

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 11:13
* **Associated Articles:** Irony (ID: `33288@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); House (ID: `realia:House`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`)

זְרֹע⁠וֹ֙ יָב֣וֹשׁ תִּיבָ֔שׁ וְ⁠עֵ֥ין יְמִינ֖⁠וֹ כָּהֹ֥ה תִכְהֶֽה

**"His arm withering will wither, and his right eye dimming will dim"**

## Zechariah - Chapter 12 Introduction (id: en_tn_ZEC_lbru)

Zechariah 12 Introduction
=========================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

This chapter is a new oracle in which Yahweh promises to defend Jerusalem and Judah against the nations that attack them (verses 1–9\), then to pour out grief so that the people mourn over “the one whom they pierced” (verses 10–14\). The repeated phrase “on that day” marks these as future events.

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### The one whom they pierced

In verse 10 the pronouns shift from “me” to “him” for the pierced one. Preserve this ambiguity rather than deciding to whom these pronouns refer.

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 12:0


## Zechariah 12:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_idoa)

מַשָּׂ֥א דְבַר־יְהוָ֖ה עַל־יִשְׂרָאֵ֑ל

**"The burden of the word of Yahweh concerning Israel"**

Zechariah is using the words translated as **The burden of the word of Yahweh concerning Israel** to introduce a new event in the story. Use a word, phrase, or other method in your language that is natural for introducing a new event.

See: Introduction of a New Event

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 12:1
* **Associated Articles:** Introduction of a New Event (ID: `33419@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Utterance (ID: `keyterm:Utterance`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`)

## Zechariah 12:2 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_qa1h)

סַף־רַ֛עַל לְ⁠כָל־הָ⁠עַמִּ֖ים סָבִ֑יב

**"a cup of staggering for all of the peoples all around"**

Here Yahweh is speaking of **Jerusalem** as if it were a **cup of staggering**, that is, a cup of wine that would make the surrounding nations drunk so that they reel and cannot stand. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[a source of helpless reeling for all of the peoples all around]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 12:2
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Under Siege (ID: `keyterm:StateOfBeingUnderSiege`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Bowl (ID: `realia:Bowl`)

## Zechariah 12:2 (#4) (id: en_tn_ZEC_axyd)

וְ⁠גַ֧ם עַל־יְהוּדָ֛ה יִֽהְיֶ֥ה בַ⁠מָּצ֖וֹר עַל־יְרוּשָׁלִָֽם

**"And also against Judah it will be, in the siege against Jerusalem"**

This could mean: (1\) Judah and Jerusalem are besieged. Alternate translation: \[And just as Jerusalem is besieged, so is all of Judah.] (2\) Judah is going to besiege Jerusalem. Alternate translation: \[Judah joins the nations to besiege Jerusalem.]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 12:2
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Under Siege (ID: `keyterm:StateOfBeingUnderSiege`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Bowl (ID: `realia:Bowl`)

## Zechariah 12:3 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_xyb9)

אֶ֤בֶן מַֽעֲמָסָה֙ לְ⁠כָל־הָ֣⁠עַמִּ֔ים

**"a stone of burden for all of the peoples"**

Here Yahweh is speaking of Jerusalem as if it were a **stone of burden**, that is, a stone so heavy that anyone who tries to lift it injures himself. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[a burden too heavy for all of the peoples]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 12:3
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`)

## Zechariah 12:3 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_qpae)

שָׂר֣וֹט יִשָּׂרֵ֑טוּ

**"injuring, will be injured"**

Yahweh is repeating forms of the verb **injuring** in order to intensify his expression of the idea that it conveys. If your language can repeat words for intensification, it would be appropriate to do that here in your translation. If not, your language may have another way of expressing the intensification. Alternate translation: \[will certainly be severely injured]

See: Reduplication

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 12:3

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`)

## Zechariah 12:3 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_lku9)

כָּל־עֹמְסֶ֖י⁠הָ

**"All of the ones lifting it"**

Yahweh is using the participle **the ones lifting it** as a noun to mean a certain kind of person, namely, the nations who try to take Jerusalem for themselves. Your language may use participles in the same way. If not, you can translate this participle with an equivalent phrase. Alternate translation: \[All of the nations who try to lift it]

See: Nominal Adjectives

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 12:3
* **Associated Articles:** Nominal Adjectives (ID: `33294@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`)

## Zechariah 12:3 (#4) (id: en_tn_ZEC_yh1s)

שָׂר֣וֹט יִשָּׂרֵ֑טוּ

**"injuring, will be injured"**

The expression **will be injured** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[will surely injure themselves severely]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 12:3
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`)

## Zechariah 12:3 (#5) (id: en_tn_ZEC_lpxq)

וְ⁠נֶאֶסְפ֣וּ עָלֶ֔י⁠הָ כֹּ֖ל גּוֹיֵ֥י הָ⁠אָֽרֶץ

**"and all of the nations of the earth will be gathered against it"**

The expression **will be gathered** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[and all of the nations of the earth will assemble themselves against it]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 12:3
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`)

## Zechariah 12:4 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_w1lt)

אַכֶּ֤ה כָל־סוּס֙ בַּ⁠תִּמָּה֔וֹן וְ⁠רֹכְב֖⁠וֹ בַּ⁠שִּׁגָּע֑וֹן

**"I will strike every horse with confusion and its rider with madness"**

If your language does not use abstract nouns for the ideas of **confusion** and **madness**, you could express the same ideas in other ways. Alternate translation: \[I will strike every horse so that it becomes bewildered and its rider so that he becomes crazed]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 12:4
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Horse (ID: `fauna:Horse`)

## Zechariah 12:4 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_hu4t)

בֵּ֤ית יְהוּדָה֙

**"the house of Judah"**

Here Yahweh is speaking of the people of Judah as if they were a **house**, a single household. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[the Judean people]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 12:4
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Horse (ID: `fauna:Horse`)

## Zechariah 12:4 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_mtfc)

אֶפְקַ֣ח אֶת־עֵינַ֔⁠י

**"I will open my eyes"**

Yahweh is using **I will open my eyes** as a common expression of the culture to mean “I will watch over and protect.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[I will keep watch protectively]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 12:4
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Horse (ID: `fauna:Horse`)

## Zechariah 12:4 (#4) (id: en_tn_ZEC_rdx0)

כָל־סוּס֙ בַּ⁠תִּמָּה֔וֹן וְ⁠רֹכְב֖⁠וֹ בַּ⁠שִּׁגָּע֑וֹן \& וְ⁠כֹל֙ ס֣וּס הָֽ⁠עַמִּ֔ים

**"every horse with confusion and its rider with madness \& and every horse of the peoples"**

Yahweh is using **every horse**, **every horse of the peoples**, and **its rider** to represent the whole army of the enemy nations. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression from your culture or state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[all the enemy forces with confusion and their soldiers with madness … every horse of the enemy]

See: Synecdoche

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 12:4
* **Associated Articles:** Synecdoche (ID: `33315@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Horse (ID: `fauna:Horse`)

## Zechariah 12:5 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_q79j)

וְ⁠אָֽמְר֛וּ אַלֻּפֵ֥י יְהוּדָ֖ה בְּ⁠לִבָּ֑⁠ם

**"And the chiefs of Judah will say in their heart"**

Zechariah is using **will say in their heart** as a common expression of the culture to mean “will say to themselves” or “will think.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[Then the chiefs of Judah will think to themselves]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 12:5
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`)

## Zechariah 12:5 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_b6rz)

אַמְצָ֥ה לִ⁠י֙ יֹשְׁבֵ֣י יְרוּשָׁלִַ֔ם בַּ⁠יהוָ֥ה צְבָא֖וֹת אֱלֹהֵי⁠הֶֽם

**"The inhabitants of Jerusalem are a strength to me by Yahweh of Armies, their God"**

The words that the chiefs of Judah say are a quotation within Yahweh’s quotation. If it would be clearer in your language, you could translate this so that there is not a quotation within a quotation. Alternate translation: \[that the dwellers of Jerusalem are strength for them through Yahweh of Armies, their God]

See: Quotes within Quotes

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 12:5
* **Associated Articles:** Quotes within Quotes (ID: `33307@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`)

## Zechariah 12:5 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_bquh)

אַמְצָ֥ה לִ⁠י֙ יֹשְׁבֵ֣י יְרוּשָׁלִַ֔ם

**"The inhabitants of Jerusalem are a strength to me"**

This could mean: (1\) the people who live in Jerusalem are strong because Yahweh of Armies their God is with them or (2\) the people who live in Jerusalem are a source of strength for the chiefs of Judah. You could make this information explicit if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[The dwellers of Jerusalem are a source of strength to me]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 12:5
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`)

## Zechariah 12:5 (#4) (id: en_tn_ZEC_ck3r)

אַמְצָ֥ה לִ⁠י֙

**"are a strength to me"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **strength**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[strengthen me]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 12:5
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`)

## Zechariah 12:5 (#5) (id: en_tn_ZEC_mfqr)

לִ⁠י֙

**"to me"**

The pronoun **me** is singular, referring to each chief of Judah individually, even though all the chiefs are speaking. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[to each of us]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 12:5
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 12:6 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_fixk)

כְּֽ⁠כִיּ֧וֹר אֵ֣שׁ בְּ⁠עֵצִ֗ים וּ⁠כְ⁠לַפִּ֥יד אֵשׁ֙ בְּ⁠עָמִ֔יר

**"like a basin of fire among trees and like a torch of fire in a sheaf"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **a basin of fire among trees** and **a torch of fire in a sheaf** quickly burn up everything around them, so the chiefs of Judah will destroy the peoples around them. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[able to consume their enemies as quickly as a firepot burns up wood and as a flaming torch burns up a sheaf of grain]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 12:6
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Sheaf (ID: `realia:Sheaf`)

## Zechariah 12:6 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_ouzo)

כְּֽ⁠כִיּ֧וֹר אֵ֣שׁ

**"like a basin of fire"**

A **basin** is a kind of large, open container that can hold burning coals or fire. If your readers would not be familiar with what a basin of this kind is, you could use the name of a similar thing that your readers would recognize, or you could use a more general term. Alternate translation: \[like a pot full of burning coals]

See: Translate Unknowns

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 12:6
* **Associated Articles:** Translate Unknowns (ID: `33408@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Sheaf (ID: `realia:Sheaf`)

## Zechariah 12:6 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_e9ek)

בְּ⁠עָמִ֔יר

**"in a sheaf"**

A **sheaf** is a bundle of cut grain stalks tied together. When dried, it is highly flammable. If your readers would not be familiar with what a sheaf is, you could use a more general term or explain it in a footnote. Alternate translation: \[bundle of cut grain]

See: Translate Unknowns

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 12:6
* **Associated Articles:** Translate Unknowns (ID: `33408@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Sheaf (ID: `realia:Sheaf`)

## Zechariah 12:6 (#4) (id: en_tn_ZEC_u4t0)

וְ⁠אָ֨כְל֜וּ

**"and they will devour"**

Here Yahweh is speaking of the chiefs of Judah destroying their enemies as if they would **devour** them the way fire consumes what it burns. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and they will destroy]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 12:6
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Judah (ID: `person:Judah.4`); Tribe of Judah (ID: `group:Judah`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Sheaf (ID: `realia:Sheaf`)

## Zechariah 12:6 (#5) (id: en_tn_ZEC_ovhs)

עַל־יָמִ֧ין וְ⁠עַל־שְׂמֹ֛אול

**"on the right and on the left"**

Yahweh is referring to every direction by naming two major components, **the right** and **the left**. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[in every direction]

See: Merism

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 12:6
* **Associated Articles:** Merism (ID: `33291@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 12:6 (#6) (id: en_tn_ZEC_dnf3)

וְ⁠יָשְׁבָ֨ה יְרוּשָׁלִַ֥ם ע֛וֹד תַּחְתֶּ֖י⁠הָ

**"And Jerusalem will dwell again in its place"**

Here, **Jerusalem** represents the people who live in Jerusalem. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[And the people of Jerusalem will dwell again in their place]

See: Metonymy

## Zechariah 12:6 (#7) (id: en_tn_ZEC_bwnj)

תַּחְתֶּ֖י⁠הָ בִּ⁠ירוּשָׁלִָֽם

**"in its place, in Jerusalem"**

It might seem that the expression **in its place, in Jerusalem** contains extra information that would not be natural to express in your language. If so, you could shorten it. Alternate translation: \[in its own place]

## Zechariah 12:7 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_por3)

לְמַ֨עַן

**"so that"**

Here, **so that** marks **the honor of the house of David and the honor of the dweller of Jerusalem will not be great over Judah** as the goal or purpose of Yahweh saving the tents of Judah first. Use a connector in your language that makes it clear that this is the purpose. Alternate translation: \[in order that]

**"the honor of the house of David and the honor of the dweller of Jerusalem"**

**"the house of David"**

Here Zechariah is speaking of the descendants of King David, the royal family, as if they were a **house**, a single household. ## Zechariah 12:7 (#5) (id: en_tn_ZEC_qtn8)

יֹשֵׁ֥ב יְרוּשָׁלִַ֖ם

**"the dweller of Jerusalem"**

Zechariah is not referring to a specific **dweller** of Jerusalem. He means the people who live in Jerusalem in general. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could use an expression that would be more natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[the residents of Jerusalem]

## Zechariah 12:7 (#6) (id: en_tn_ZEC_r5l8)

לֹֽא־תִגְדַּ֜ל תִּפְאֶ֣רֶת בֵּית־דָּוִ֗יד וְ⁠תִפְאֶ֛רֶת יֹשֵׁ֥ב יְרוּשָׁלִַ֖ם עַל־יְהוּדָֽה

**"the honor of the house of David and the honor of the dweller of Jerusalem will not be great over Judah"**

Zechariah is using **will not be great over Judah** as a common expression of the culture to mean “will not be considered more important than the rest of Judah.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[the honor of the house of David and the honor of the dweller of Jerusalem will not be regarded as more important than the honor of the rest of Judah]

See: Idiom

הַ⁠נִּכְשָׁ֥ל בָּ⁠הֶ֛ם

**"and the stumbler among them"**

Here Zechariah is speaking of a weak or feeble person as if he were a **stumbler**. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and the weakest one among them]

## Zechariah 12:8 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_b232)

הַ⁠נִּכְשָׁ֥ל

**"and the stumbler"**

Zechariah is using the adjective **stumbler** as a noun to mean a certain kind of person. Your language may use adjectives in the same way. If not, you can translate this adjective with an equivalent phrase. Alternate translation: \[the one who stumbles]

See: Nominal Adjectives

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 12:8
* **Associated Articles:** Nominal Adjectives (ID: `33294@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 12:8 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_qeir)

וְ⁠הָיָ֞ה \& כְּ⁠דָוִ֑יד וּ⁠בֵ֤ית דָּוִיד֙ כֵּֽ⁠אלֹהִ֔ים

**"will be like David, and the house of David, like God"**

Zechariah says **will be like David, and the house of David, like God** as an extreme statement for emphasis. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use a different way to express the emphasis. Alternate translation: \[will be strong like David was, and the house of David will seem as powerful as God]

See: Hyperbole

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 12:8
* **Associated Articles:** Hyperbole (ID: `33281@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 12:8 (#4) (id: en_tn_ZEC_auur)

וּ⁠בֵ֤ית דָּוִיד֙ כֵּֽ⁠אלֹהִ֔ים

**"and the house of David, like God"**

Zechariah is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. It may be helpful to repeat them from the earlier phrase. Alternate translation: \[and the house of David will be like God]

## Zechariah 12:8 (#5) (id: en_tn_ZEC_qgyu)

וּ⁠בֵ֤ית דָּוִיד֙ כֵּֽ⁠אלֹהִ֔ים כְּ⁠מַלְאַ֥ךְ יְהוָ֖ה

**"and the house of David, like God, like the angel of Yahweh"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **God** and **the angel of Yahweh** are powerful, so the house of David will be powerful as they lead the people. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[and the house of David will be as powerful as God is, as powerful as the angel of Yahweh]

כְּ⁠מַלְאַ֥ךְ יְהוָ֖ה לִ⁠פְנֵי⁠הֶֽם

**"like the angel of Yahweh to their face"**

Here, **their face** represents their presence, so the phrase means going ahead of the people to lead them. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[like having the angel of Yahweh fighting in front of them]

וְ⁠הָיָ֖ה בַּ⁠יּ֣וֹם הַ⁠ה֑וּא אֲבַקֵּ֗שׁ לְ⁠הַשְׁמִיד֙ אֶת־כָּל־הַ⁠גּוֹיִ֔ם הַ⁠בָּאִ֖ים עַל־יְרוּשָׁלִָֽם

## Zechariah 12:10 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_l6tl)

וְ⁠הִבִּ֥יטוּ אֵלַ֖⁠י אֵ֣ת אֲשֶׁר־דָּקָ֑רוּ וְ⁠סָפְד֣וּ עָלָ֗י⁠ו

**"and they will look to me, whom they pierced. And they will mourn over him"**

The pronoun **him** refers to the same person as **me** earlier in the verse. Yahweh shifts here from speaking of himself in the first person to speaking of the pierced one in the third person. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[and they will look to me, whom they pierced. And they will mourn over the one they pierced]

וְ⁠סָפְד֣וּ עָלָ֗י⁠ו כְּ⁠מִסְפֵּד֙ עַל־הַ⁠יָּחִ֔יד

**"And they will mourn over him as the mourning over the only one"**

The point of this comparison is that just as people grieve very deeply for **the only one**, that is, an only child who has died, so the people will mourn over the one whom they pierced. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[And they will mourn over him as deeply as people grieve for an only child who has died]

**"and they will be bitter over him as being bitter over the firstborn"**

The point of this comparison is that just as people grieve bitterly over **the firstborn**, that is, a firstborn son who has died, so the people will grieve bitterly over the one whom they pierced. ## Zechariah 12:11 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_ya2v)

כְּ⁠מִסְפַּ֥ד הֲדַדְ־רִמּ֖וֹן בְּ⁠בִקְעַ֥ת מְגִדּֽוֹן

**"like the mourning of Hadad Rimmon in the plain of Megiddo"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **the mourning of Hadad Rimmon in the plain of Megiddo** was a well\-known time of very great grief, so the mourning in Jerusalem will be very great. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[as great as the well\-known grief at Hadad Rimmon in the plain of Megiddo]

**"clans, clans separately"**

Zechariah is repeating the term **clans** in order to intensify his expression of the idea that it conveys, that each clan mourns by itself. ## Zechariah 12:12–14 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_ya2b)

מִשְׁפַּ֨חַת בֵּית־דָּוִ֤יד לְ⁠בָד֙ וּ⁠נְשֵׁי⁠הֶ֣ם לְ⁠בָ֔ד

**"the clan of the house of David separately, and their wives separately"**

In some languages, the repeated systematic repetition of the rest of verse 12 through verse 14 may not give a similar emphasis to the deepness and powerfulness of the mourning that will happen. Translate in a way that emphasizes how strong the grief was in a natural way in your language. Alternate translation: \[the clans of David, Nathan, Levi, and the Shimeite will all mourn very intensely; the men and women will mourn separately, in their own ways]

* **Associated Articles:** Information Structure (ID: `33286@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 12:12–14 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_ew7z)

מִשְׁפַּ֨חַת בֵּית־דָּוִ֤יד לְ⁠בָד֙ וּ⁠נְשֵׁי⁠הֶ֣ם לְ⁠בָ֔ד

**"the clan of the house of David separately, and their wives separately"**

Zechariah assumes that readers will know that the separation of men and women in public mourning reflects the mourning customs of that society. Translate this so readers do not misinterpret it as marital strife or divorce. You could say that explicitly if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[According to custom, each clan will mourn by itself, and men and women will mourn separately]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationNotes`)

**"all of the clans, the ones remaining"**

Zechariah is using the adjective **the ones remaining** as a noun to mean certain clans, namely, all the other clans that have not yet been named. Your language may use adjectives in the same way. ## Zechariah - Chapter 13 Introduction (id: en_tn_ZEC_uxpd)

Zechariah 13 Introduction
=========================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

Verses 1–6 are a prose oracle about cleansing from sin and removing false prophets. Verses 7–9 shift to poetry, announcing the striking of Yahweh’s shepherd and the purifying of a surviving remnant.

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### The Prophetic Cloak

The “cloak of hair” (verse 4\) is the rough garment that identified prophets. False prophets abandon this clothing to conceal their identity.

Translation Issues in This Chapter
----------------------------------

### Nested Quotations

Yahweh’s speech spans the entire chapter and contains embedded quotes from parents (verse 3\), a false prophet (verses 5–6\), and a covenant declaration (verse 9\).

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 13:0


## Zechariah 13:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_fa7t)

בַּ⁠יּ֣וֹם הַ⁠ה֗וּא

**"On that day"**

Zechariah is using the phrase **On that day** to introduce a new event in the story. Use a word, phrase, or other method in your language that is natural for introducing a new event. See the Introduction to Zechariah for a discussion of the similar phrase “in that day.”

See: Introduction of a New Event

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 13:1
* **Associated Articles:** Introduction of a New Event (ID: `33419@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`); David (ID: `person:David`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`)

Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[a source of cleansing will be made available]

The expression **a fountain will be opened** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. If you need to say who does the action, the context indicates that it is God. Alternate translation: \[God will open a fountain]

## Zechariah 13:2 (#5) (id: en_tn_ZEC_in59)

וְ⁠גַ֧ם

**"and also"**

Here, the words **and also** add a further action to the one just described. Yahweh will not only remove the idols but will additionally remove the false prophets and the unclean spirit. If the connection between this statement and the previous one is not clear, you may want to use a different connecting word to show how this statement relates to what comes before it. Alternate translation: \[and furthermore,]

See: Connecting Words and Phrases

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 13:2
* **Associated Articles:** Connecting Words and Phrases (ID: `33335@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 13:3 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_wtof)

וְ⁠הָיָ֗ה כִּֽי־יִנָּבֵ֣א אִישׁ֮ עוֹד֒

**"And it will happen, when a man prophesies again"**

Zechariah is presenting a possible condition, that a man will prophesy falsely again and, as a result, his own father and mother will condemn and kill him. Use a natural form in your language for introducing a condition that could happen. It may be helpful to reorder this sentence so that the conditional statement is first. Alternate translation: \[If a man prophesies falsely again]

See: Connect — Hypothetical Conditions

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 13:3
* **Associated Articles:** Connect — Hypothetical Conditions (ID: `33327@UWTranslationNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Speak as a Prophet (ID: `keyterm:Speak`)

## Zechariah 13:3 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_k937)

אִישׁ֮

**"a man"**

Zechariah is not referring to a specific **man**. He means men in general who prophesy. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could use an expression that would be more natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[any man] or \[anyone]

יֹֽלְדָי⁠ו֙

**"the ones bearing him"**

Zechariah is not distinguishing this father and mother from another father and mother who are not **the ones bearing him**. He is reminding readers that the man’s own parents are the ones who gave birth to him. Be sure that this is clear in your translation. Alternate translation: \[who themselves gave him birth]

## Zechariah 13:5 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_a2yx)

אֲדָמָה֙ \& כִּ֥י אָדָ֖ם הִקְנַ֥⁠נִי מִ⁠נְּעוּרָֽ⁠י

**"the ground, for a man has owned me from my youth"**

This could mean: (1\) that the person is tied to this land and working this land because he is a bondservant or slave to the owner. Alternate translation: \[this ground that my master from my youth owns] (2\) that he owns this land (requires reading the Hebrew slightly differently). וְ⁠אָמַ֣ר אֵלָ֔י⁠ו מָ֧ה הַ⁠מַּכּ֛וֹת הָ⁠אֵ֖לֶּה בֵּ֣ין יָדֶ֑י⁠ךָ וְ⁠אָמַ֕ר אֲשֶׁ֥ר הֻכֵּ֖יתִי בֵּ֥ית מְאַהֲבָֽ⁠י

## Zechariah 13:6 (#4) (id: en_tn_ZEC_xeyl)

בֵּ֥ית מְאַהֲבָֽ⁠י

**"in} the house of my lovers"**

This could mean: (1\) that the speaker is saying he was injured somehow while at the home of friends, perhaps in a drunken brawl or something. Alternate translation: \[when I got in a fight with some local people] (2\) that the speaker is speaking of the false gods that he worshiped as if they were his **lovers**. Idol worship sometimes involves self\-mutilation. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[in the temples of the false gods I worshiped]

## Zechariah 13:7 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_inib)

חֶ֗רֶב עוּרִ֤י עַל־רֹעִ⁠י֙

**"Sword, awake against my shepherd"**

Yahweh is speaking to a **Sword** that he knows cannot hear him. He is doing this to communicate to the people listening to him that he feels very strongly about the judgment that is coming on his shepherd. If it would be helpful in your language, you could translate these words as if he were speaking directly to the people about the sword. Alternate translation: \[May the sword awake against my shepherd]

## Zechariah 13:8 (#1) (id: en_tn_ZEC_u4ir)

פִּֽי־שְׁנַ֣יִם בָּ֔⁠הּ יִכָּרְת֖וּ יִגְוָ֑עוּ וְ⁠הַ⁠שְּׁלִשִׁ֖ית יִוָּ֥תֶר בָּֽ⁠הּ

**"two portions in it will be cut off, they will expire, and the third will be left in it"**

The expression **two portions** means two parts out of three equal parts, that is, two\-thirds of the people. And **the third** means one part out of three equal parts, that is, one\-third of the people.

## Zechariah 13:9 (#2) (id: en_tn_ZEC_aapj)

וּ⁠צְרַפְתִּי⁠ם֙ כִּ⁠צְרֹ֣ף אֶת־הַ⁠כֶּ֔סֶף וּ⁠בְחַנְתִּ֖י⁠ם כִּ⁠בְחֹ֣ן אֶת־הַ⁠זָּהָ֑ב

**"and I will refine them as {one} refines the silver, and I will test them as {one} tests the gold"**

The point of this comparison is that just as one **refines the silver** and **tests the gold** to remove impurities and prove them genuine, so Yahweh will purify his people through suffering and prove that they are genuine. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[and I will purify them the way that one refines silver to remove its impurities, and I will prove them genuine the way that one tests gold]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 13:9
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 13:9 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_uf95)

ה֣וּא

**"He"**

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 13:9
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 13:9 (#4) (id: en_tn_ZEC_abzy)

ה֣וּא ׀ יִקְרָ֣א בִ⁠שְׁמִ֗⁠י וַֽ⁠אֲנִי֙ אֶעֱנֶ֣ה

**"He, he will call on my name, and I, I will answer"**

Yahweh is stating the pronouns **He** and **I** along with the verbs translated as **he will call** and **I will answer**, which already include their meanings. He is doing that because he is contrasting what the remnant of his people will do with what he himself will do. If a speaker of your language would use explicit pronouns for the same purpose, you may want to use that construction in your translation. If not, your language may have other ways of showing that meaning here. Alternate translation: \[As for them, they will call on my name, and as for me, I will answer him]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 13:9
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationNotes`)

## Zechariah 13:9 (#6) (id: en_tn_ZEC_chmo)

עַמִּ֣⁠י ה֔וּא \& יְהוָ֥ה אֱלֹהָֽ⁠י

**"He is my people \& Yahweh is my God"**

The implication is that **He is my people** and **Yahweh is my God** are the words of a covenant, expressing the restored relationship in which Yahweh claims these people as his own and they acknowledge him as their God. You could include this information if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[He is the people I claim as my own … Yahweh is the one I acknowledge as my God]

## Zechariah - Chapter 14 Introduction (id: en_tn_ZEC_zstv)

Zechariah 14 Introduction
=========================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

This closing chapter is a prose oracle about the day of Yahweh: the nations attack Jerusalem (verses 1–8\), Yahweh reigns as king (verses 9–11\), he strikes the attackers (verses 12–15\), and the survivors come to worship him (verses 16–21\).

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### The climax of the book

Zechariah 14:20–21 presents a picture of universal holiness, where ordinary domestic objects carry the same sacredness as the High Priest’s crown. In Exodus 28:36, this inscription belonged exclusively to the High Priest’s golden crown. Here it is stamped on common horse bells and kitchen pots, signifying that in the messianic kingdom, the boundary between the sacred and the everyday is abolished, and all of life and creation becomes completely holy to God. Compare this to Isaiah 11:9 and Habakkuk 2:14, where the holy presence and knowledge of God fill the entire earth.

### The day of Yahweh

The repeated phrase “on that day” marks the time when Yahweh judges the nations and establishes his rule. See \[\[rc://\*/tw/dict/bible/kt/dayofthelord]].

### The Festival of Booths

This was a yearly Israelite festival during which people lived in temporary shelters. Here even the nations celebrate it.

Translation Issues in This Chapter
----------------------------------

### Shifts in person

Yahweh speaks of himself as “I” in verse 2 but is spoken of as “he” elsewhere, so translators may need to make the speaker explicit.

* **Associated Passages:** Zechariah 14:0

Zechariah is using the term **Behold** to focus the attention of his listeners on what he is about to say. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[Listen carefully]

## Zechariah 14:1 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_tklr)

וְ⁠חֻלַּ֥ק שְׁלָלֵ֖⁠ךְ בְּ⁠קִרְבֵּֽ⁠ךְ

**"and your spoil will be divided in your midst"**

Zechariah is speaking to the city of Jerusalem, which he knows cannot hear him. He is doing this to communicate to the people listening to him that he feels very strongly about what will happen to the city. If it would be helpful in your language, you could translate these words as if he were speaking directly to the people about Jerusalem. Alternate translation: \[and the spoil taken from Jerusalem will be divided within that city]

## Zechariah 14:6 (#3) (id: en_tn_ZEC_zlqi)
